    cs.CV cs.AI

    Luggage re-identification: A study of strategies towards a real-world application

    Authors: Quentin Dhôte, Romaric Audigier

    Abstract: …background and crowded scenes which significantly degrade performance. Yet, strategies leveraging the complementarity of multiple shots and views from different video streams is overlooked. In this paper, we study strategies for an end-to-end pipeline that operates from multiple video streams to detect and re-identify… ▽ More Luggage re-identification (ReID) is a critical task in video surveillance for forensics, security or border management (suspicious or abandoned luggage recognition), as well as luggage management (lost luggage recovery). It enables the association of observations of the same luggage across disjoint camera views in complex environments such as airports, train stations and border crossing. While existing approaches primarily focus on appearance-based cropped image matching under relatively clean conditions, real-world scenarios are characterized by frequent occlusions, cluttered background and crowded scenes which significantly degrade performance. Yet, strategies leveraging the complementarity of multiple shots and views from different video streams is overlooked. In this paper, we study strategies for an end-to-end pipeline that operates from multiple video streams to detect and re-identify luggage. We evaluate different variants on a benchmark dataset acquired in real-world environments to address the questions of selecting and merging different views and shots of the same luggage as well as training on different environment datasets for an overall ReID performance improvement in practical applications. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    Comments: 7 pages, 4 figures, 5 tables. Accepted at The 2026 IEEE International Conference on Content-Based Multimedia Indexing (IEEE CBMI)

  2. arXiv:2610.40636  [pdf, ps, other] 

    cs.CV cs.AI

    Probing Spatial State Representations in Visual Foundation Models

    Authors: Baiqiao Yin, Zhongang Cai, Ruisi Wang, Qiwei Liang, Lei Yang, Ziwei Liu

    Abstract: …levels of spatial capability---spatial structure, object persistence, predictive spatial update, and actionability---using frozen-feature probes on ten representations under streaming egocentric observations of static indoor environments. The resulting capability profiles vary across tasks, network layers, and prefix lengths: strong readout of the visible sc… ▽ More An observer sees only part of the surrounding world, yet must keep track of what lies beyond the current view. Spatial perception therefore involves more than recognizing individual images: it calls for a persistent spatial state that is updated under self-motion and used to guide action. Do visual foundation models provide representations that support such a state? We study this question through four levels of spatial capability---spatial structure, object persistence, predictive spatial update, and actionability---using frozen-feature probes on ten representations under streaming egocentric observations of static indoor environments. The resulting capability profiles vary across tasks, network layers, and prefix lengths: strong readout of the visible scene does not ensure accurate object persistence or future-feature retrieval. Matched controls separate readout accuracy from reliance on visual history and motion. Native future prediction retains recoverable spatial structure, but a spatial reader trained on real features does not transfer to predicted features unchanged. Finally, goal-dependent candidate selection does not establish reliable continuous action utility: predicted actions respond to goal changes, yet supplying the correct goal does not reliably improve progress. Across the four levels, object persistence and actionability build on camera-pose readout, whereas feature prediction builds on visible-scene readout. Together, these findings motivate evaluating spatial representations not only by what can be decoded from them, but by whether that information is maintained beyond observation, updated predictively, and used for goal-directed action. △ Less

    cs.RO cs.CV

    SymAnt: Grounding Long-Horizon Action Anticipation with Adaptable Symbolic Priors

    Authors: Fnu Aryan, Yaqi Xie, Joseph Campbell, Woojun Kim, Kwonjoon Lee, Hossein Nourkhiz Mahjoub, Ehsan Moradi-Pari, Katia Sycara, Simon Stepputtis

    Abstract: As robots and intelligent systems increasingly interact with humans, they must anticipate long… ▽ More As robots and intelligent systems increasingly interact with humans, they must anticipate long sequences of future actions from only brief observations. While purely neural models typically require extensive observations to predict future actions and remain difficult to interpret and adjust, symbolic priors offer a structured alternative. To this end, we introduce SymAnt, a novel neuro-symbolic approach that unifies the expressiveness of neural networks with the structured reasoning of scene and knowledge graphs. Given a short video, SymAnt performs a joint-search over both graphs to construct a symbolic context, enabling unified reasoning over spatial relationships, object attributes, affordances, and associated actions. This symbolic context serves as a prior for an iterative refinement process that progressively generates and improves the sequence of future actions. By grounding predictions in symbolic context, SymAnt anticipates long-horizon actions from short observation windows in a parameter-efficient manner, while enabling users to directly edit the underlying knowledge to adjust predictions without further training. Through our experiments, we demonstrate the effectiveness of SymAnt, particularly when only short observation contexts are available, across five diverse datasets: Breakfast, 50 Salads, EPIC-KITCHENS, EGTEA Gaze+, and Assembly101. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    Comments: Accepted to WACV 2027. 18 pages (8-page main paper, references, and supplementary appendix), 6 figures, 9 tables. Project page: https://aryanmangal769.github.io/symant/

  4. arXiv:2610.40614  [pdf, ps, other] 

    cs.CV

    L3G-World: Real-Time Infinite World Generation with Latent 3D Gaussian Memory

    Authors: Jinhyeok Choi, JoungBin Lee, Jongmin Lee, Minkyeong Jeon, Jiyoung Kim, Kaeun Han, Yoonjin Yoon, Seungryong Kim

    Abstract: Interactive world models generate visual content conditioned on user actions, yet maintaining spatial consistency over long-… ▽ More Interactive world models generate visual content conditioned on user actions, yet maintaining spatial consistency over long-horizon exploration in real time remains challenging. Existing approaches rely on either temporal context or explicit 3D memory, both of which scale poorly because memory grows with the length of the generated stream. Moreover, explicit 3D memories condition latent video generators by re-encoding RGB renderings, which precludes real-time generation. In this paper, we introduce a Latent 3D Gaussian Memory (L3G) that combines the efficiency of feed-forward reconstruction with a compact, rapidly renderable scene representation. Building on this memory, we propose L3G-World, in which a memory updater and a video diffusion transformer (DiT) alternate autoregressively to generate spatially consistent worlds. The memory updater spawns Gaussians in newly observed regions and refines existing ones, so that the memory grows with scene extent rather than stream length. Each Gaussian further stores a VAE latent feature, allowing the memory to be rendered directly into the DiT's latent space without VAE re-encoding. Experiments show that L3G-World achieves state-of-the-art generation quality and long-horizon spatial consistency, with up to $38.6\times$ fewer 3D primitives, $3.2\times$ faster memory updates, and $359\times$ faster memory rendering relative to the most efficient prior 3D-memory baseline for each metric, enabling real-time world generation at 9 FPS. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    Comments: Project page: https://cvlab-kaist.github.io/L3G-World/

    HairDeck: Animation-Ready Stylized Hair Cards from a Single Image

    Authors: Yuze He, Yixiang Wei, Wang Zhao, Chuhao Chen, Yanning Zhou, Yong-Jin Liu

    Abstract: …Existing image-based methods produce realistic hair or a single fused mesh, while CHARM, the only generator of stylized hair cards, decodes a hairstyle autoregressively as a long sequence of control points. Observing that a card hairstyle is an unordered set of artist-defined primitives, we propose HairDeck, which gen… ▽ More The hair of stylized characters in games and avatars is built from hair cards: artist-placed ribbons whose geometry is the visible hair, animated by spring bones. Existing image-based methods produce realistic hair or a single fused mesh, while CHARM, the only generator of stylized hair cards, decodes a hairstyle autoregressively as a long sequence of control points. Observing that a card hairstyle is an unordered set of artist-defined primitives, we propose HairDeck, which generates it as one: each card is a single token, and a set diffusion transformer denoises all cards together. From a single image, HairDeck generates the cards and the spring-bone chains that drive them, yielding an animatable asset that exports to ready-to-use downstream formats. On CHARM's own test set, it lowers Chamfer distance by 44% and card-orientation error by more than half while running 33 times faster, and from single images it outperforms CHARM, image-to-3D pipelines and strand-based methods. Project page: https://hyzcluster.github.io/HairDeck △ Less

    cs.RO

    DAWN: Diffusion-based Alignment and unfastening Workflow Network

    Authors: Hemant Kumar, Piyush Goenka, Nikhil Chopra

    Abstract: …challenges in precise screwdriver engagement with fasteners, detailed force execution, and robustness to environmental variability. To address the complexity of learning long-horizon, contact-rich manipulation tasks, we subdivide the unfastening task procedure into two sequential stages: (1) alignment & insertion,… ▽ More We present DAWN (Diffusion-based Alignment and unfastening Workflow Network), a hierarchical robotic framework designed to perform real-world, unfastening tasks through multi-modal sensory integration. Autonomous robotic unfastening is crucial for improving recycling processes, enhancing safety, and reducing operational costs; yet, it remains predominantly manual due to inherent challenges in precise screwdriver engagement with fasteners, detailed force execution, and robustness to environmental variability. To address the complexity of learning long-horizon, contact-rich manipulation tasks, we subdivide the unfastening task procedure into two sequential stages: (1) alignment & insertion, and (2) unfastening. DAWN consists of three key components: AlignNet, a diffusion-based policy ensuring precise screwdriver tip engagement; LatchNet, a multi-modal classifier reliably detecting successful stage transitions; and UnfastNet, which achieves stable, force-controlled screw removal. Experimental validation on a Franka Emika robot demonstrates that DAWN achieves alignment & insertion success rates comparable to trained human operators, robustly transitions between stages, and attains an impressive success rate in unfastening across various unseen scenarios. Ablation studies further elucidate critical input modalities and effective training strategies, highlighting DAWN's scalability, robustness, and data efficiency. △ Less

    math.NA cs.LG physics.comp-ph physics.plasm-ph

    NEOPIC: A Neural Operator Framework for Particle-based Kinetic Plasma Simulations

    Authors: Sriramkrishnan Muralikrishnan, Chelsea Maria John, Jeffrey Kelling, Michael Bussmann, Robert Speck, Paul Gibbon

    Abstract: …set by the architecture alone, independently of any mesh or tree. Numerical experiments on electrostatic benchmarks in two and three dimensions, including Landau damping, two-stream and bump-on-tail instabilities, a cyclotron mini-app and a Penning trap, confirm the theoretical findings. A single model trained on several mini-apps generated by different refe… ▽ More We introduce NEOPIC, a framework for learning the field operator in particle-based methods applied to kinetic plasma simulations. Its key novelty is a neural operator that acts directly on the empirical measure of the particles, rather than on a grid, and is evaluated at the particle positions. No mesh or tree is needed and, unlike grid-based neural operators, no deposition onto a mesh takes place, so the particle noise that dominates the inputs of particle simulations is not aliased. Since it replaces only the field solve, it serves as a plug-and-play field solver for particle-based methods: it can be trained on data from particle-in-cell, particle-in-Fourier or tree codes, and combined with any time integrator, including implicit schemes. We prove that the discrete evaluation coincides with the continuous operator at any number of particles (representation equivalence) and derive an error analysis that separates sampling, learning and time-stepping errors: up to the learning error, NEOPIC has the error structure of standard Monte Carlo particle methods. We show that the particle noise in the training data acts as an implicit regularizer, which gives guidance on the number of training particles in relation to the particle numbers used at inference. A complexity analysis shows that NEOPIC is an $O(N_p)$ method whose constants are set by the architecture alone, independently of any mesh or tree. Numerical experiments on electrostatic benchmarks in two and three dimensions, including Landau damping, two-stream and bump-on-tail instabilities, a cyclotron mini-app and a Penning trap, confirm the theoretical findings. A single model trained on several mini-apps generated by different reference methods captures all of them and remains stable up to twice the training time window, which points towards foundation models for fields in particle-based methods. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    Comments: 84 pages, 19 figures, 3 tables. Code: https://github.com/srikrrish/operator_learning (tags neopic-arxiv-v1, neopic-arxiv-v1-nufft); analysis scripts: https://github.com/NEOPIC-framework/neopic-paper-scripts

    cs.CV

    GameVerse: A Minute-Scale Gameplay Dataset for Long-Horizon Interactive World Modeling

    Authors: Kang He, Wenshuo Peng, Chuanhao Li, Zihui Gao, Xiaojie Xu, Zhengyuan Lin, Yuzhe Ding, Donghong Ji, Kaipeng Zhang, Yongtao Ge

    Abstract: …from action inputs. Yet current models struggle to maintain coherence beyond roughly one minute of rollout, a limitation rooted not in model capacity but in the scarcity of long-… ▽ More Recent advances in interactive world models have made it possible to generate controllable, dynamic environments from action inputs. Yet current models struggle to maintain coherence beyond roughly one minute of rollout, a limitation rooted not in model capacity but in the scarcity of long-horizon training data. Existing gameplay video datasets typically consist of clips under ten seconds, with captions that describe whole clips in coarse and undifferentiated text, which is insufficient for controllable long-horizon generation. We address this with GameVerse, a minute-scale gameplay video dataset purpose-built for long-horizon interactive world modeling. GameVerse contains 69.7K clips spanning 1,655 hours and 349.6 million frames across diverse first- and third-person gameplay, including 1,358 hours of continuous two-minute sequences, an order of magnitude longer than prior datasets. Clips pass through a unified pipeline of scene-consistent segmentation, quality filtering, camera pose estimation, and event-based caption annotation. Each clip is paired with a temporally stable camera trajectory that remains coherent across minutes, together with captions that separate static scene context from dynamic foreground events and align to event boundaries rather than fixed time windows. Comprehensive analysis shows that GameVerse surpasses existing public datasets in scale, diversity, and annotation quality. Experiments further demonstrate that training on GameVerse substantially extends the temporal horizon of interactive world models. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; v1 submitted 27 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    Comments: Accepted by NeurIPS 2026

    physics.comp-ph

    Predicting Weight Windows for Duct-Streaming Shielding Geometries with a Convolutional Neural Network

    Authors: Jilang Miao, Ansel Kneier, Jonathan Porrazzo

    Abstract: Forward-flux-based weight windows give global variance reduction for duct-streaming shielding problems, but each new geometry needs an iterative Monte Carlo calculation to build them. We report preliminary results on a convolutional neural network (CNN) that learns the map from a two-dimensional duct geometry and source position to the lower window bound of… ▽ More Forward-flux-based weight windows give global variance reduction for duct-streaming shielding problems, but each new geometry needs an iterative Monte Carlo calculation to build them. We report preliminary results on a convolutional neural network (CNN) that learns the map from a two-dimensional duct geometry and source position to the lower window bound of such an iteration, here the MAGIC generator in OpenMC, in milliseconds. A set of 250 random concrete ducts of three curved and rectangular families on the Protvino labyrinth bounding box was run in OpenMC with a Cf-252 point source and MAGIC generation, and a 1.9-million-parameter U-Net was trained on the logarithm of the lower bound. On held-out ducts the error in the cells that carry the flux is 0.14 to 0.51 decades; replicate MAGIC runs show this part of the map to be reproducible to a few hundredths of a decade, so the residual is model error, while below $10^{-6}$ the reference itself is noise. Applied without retraining to the rectangular Protvino labyrinth, the prediction correlates with MAGIC at 0.96. Used as weight windows there, with its deepest cells clamped at deployment, it gives an exit-detector figure of merit 54 times the analog value, 1.7 times that of the MAGIC generation run for the same geometry, and 0.80 of that of reused MAGIC windows whose construction it does not pay for. The run cost of any window set is shown to be set by its deepest cells and predictable from a population proxy. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    Comments: 11 pages, 3 figures, 5 tables. Submitted to M&C 2027, the International Conference on Mathematics and Computational Methods Applied to Nuclear Science and Engineering (American Nuclear Society)

Building on these data, Geometry-Guided Adaptation couples a VGGT geometry stream with image and text streams under target-scene feature supervision to guide spatially consistent insertion. Complementary 2D Object-Scene Spatial Superv… ▽ More We present PhysInsert, a framework for physically plausible reference-based object insertion that jointly adapts objects and their surrounding scenes. Existing methods often rely on masked inputs, generation-based pairs, or training pairs with limited viewpoint variation, leaving pose and illumination adaptation and bidirectional object-scene interactions insufficiently constrained. We construct the Insert World dataset with strictly aligned Blender-rendered counterfactual pairs and object references across viewpoints and lighting conditions. Building on these data, Geometry-Guided Adaptation couples a VGGT geometry stream with image and text streams under target-scene feature supervision to guide spatially consistent insertion. Complementary 2D Object-Scene Spatial Supervision jointly supervises object occupancy and environmental changes while grounding spatial instructions in object regions through image-text attention. We introduce PhysInsert-Bench with dedicated cross-environment and cross-view cases requiring illumination and pose adaptation beyond appearance copying. Experiments on PhysInsert-Bench and DreamBooth demonstrate improved image fidelity; a user study further shows more accurate placement and improved lighting integration, perspective, and pose plausibility. △ Less

  11. arXiv:2610.40350  [pdf, ps, other] 

    cs.CL

    Don't Lose the Thread: Empowering Long-Horizon LLM Agents with Cognitive Resource Self-Allocation

    Authors: Zheng Zhu, Xiaozhuan Liang, Xi Chen, Bei Yu, Jiaya Jia

    Abstract: Agents powered by large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable progress in solving complex reasoning tasks. However, LLM agents often falter on long-… ▽ More Agents powered by large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable progress in solving complex reasoning tasks. However, LLM agents often falter on long-horizon tasks due to cognitive overload, as their working memory becomes cluttered with expanding and irrelevant information, which dilutes their attention and hinders effective planning and reasoning. To mitigate this challenge, we introduce Cognitive Resource Self-Allocation (CORAL), a novel reasoning paradigm that empowers agents to proactively optimize their context. Implemented as an agent-callable working memory management toolset, CORAL allows an agent to checkpoint its task progress and verified facts, and adaptively initiate a new problem-solving episode by resetting cluttered context while preserving only the checkpointed information, enabling it to resume reasoning from a clean state. We further enhance the agent's checkpointing capabilities using a Multi-episode Agentic Reinforced Policy Optimization algorithm. On the GAIA benchmark, CORAL with an 8B model achieves 42.8% average accuracy, with particularly strong gains on the most challenging long-horizon tasks (Level 2 and Level 3). Crucially, these gains come at a substantially lower inference cost: CORAL consumes 6-7$\times$ fewer input tokens than a ReAct agent. It also generalizes to multi-hop Knowledge QA benchmarks and to the considerably harder BrowseComp, where it improves a strong proprietary agent by 8.0 points. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    Comments: Accepted at COLM 2026. Code is available at https://github.com/JIA-Lab-research/CORAL

    cs.LG cs.AI cs.RO eess.SY

    Learned Terminal Costs for Short-Horizon Model Predictive Control of Autonomous Trucks in Interactive Highway Traffic

    Authors: Hooman Hamze, Benjamin Houshmand, Deepthi Pathare, Erik Börve, Morteza Haghir Chehreghani, Nikolce Murgovski

    Abstract: …intricate dynamics increase the requirements for cooperation from surrounding traffic in critical maneuvers, requiring a careful balance between safety constraints and efficient long-horizon decision making. Model Predictive Control (MPC) provides rigorous constraint satisfaction but relies on terminal cost which can l… ▽ More Autonomous highway driving for heavy-duty trucks is a challenging control problem. The greater mass, dimensions and intricate dynamics increase the requirements for cooperation from surrounding traffic in critical maneuvers, requiring a careful balance between safety constraints and efficient long-horizon decision making. Model Predictive Control (MPC) provides rigorous constraint satisfaction but relies on terminal cost which can limit its effectiveness at short, computationally affordable horizons. Reinforcement Learning (RL) captures long-term driving behavior but offers no constraint guarantees and tends toward overly conservative policies when trained under sparse collision penalties. In this paper, we propose a hybrid MPC and RL framework for highway driving in autonomous trucks, where a finite-horizon MPC, solved via dynamic programming, uses a pre-trained RL model for terminal cost estimation. We evaluate the framework in a simulation environment with reactive surrounding traffic and compare it against pure MPC with zero terminal cost, rollout with terminal cost estimation using a base policy, and pure RL. Results show that the hybrid approach achieves zero collisions with the lowest cost of any configuration. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

    Comments: 13 pages, 2 figures, 8 tables

    ACM Class: I.2.6; I.2.8

    cs.SE cs.CL

    When Requirements Interact: Scaling Long-Horizon Coding via Interdependent Specifications

    Authors: Yishuo Cai, Minzheng Wang, Liang Zhao, Linghao Zhang, Lei Li, Shicheng Li, Xu Sun, Fuli Luo

    Abstract: To succeed in real-world long-horizon software development, coding agents must satisfy multi-dimensional task specifications, such as interface compatibility and error handling, and do so without breaking existing dependencies. However, current works lack specification in both quantity and diversity, often emphasizing… ▽ More To succeed in real-world long-horizon software development, coding agents must satisfy multi-dimensional task specifications, such as interface compatibility and error handling, and do so without breaking existing dependencies. However, current works lack specification in both quantity and diversity, often emphasizing primary goals while ignoring complex constraints. Synthesizing tasks to bridge this gap presents two challenges: naively adding specifications risks reducing tasks to trivial checklists rather than compelling joint reasoning, and verifying these subtle interactions risks overlooking hidden flaws or rejecting valid alternatives. To address these challenges, we introduce CodeSpecWeave, a framework for synthesizing tasks with rich, interdependent specifications. Grounded in real developer seeds, a synthesis agent iteratively develops both coupled task requirements and executable tests, while a review agent audits solution trajectories from multiple models to guarantee test reliability. Using this pipeline, we construct 4,670 training tasks across 2,865 repositories. Applying Reinforcement learning on these tasks significantly improves performance over the base model on DeepSWE-v1.1, largely outperforming existing baselines. Furthermore, we curate CodeSpecWeave-Bench, a rigorous benchmark comprising 100 tasks and 1,044 specifications, which clearly separates frontier models and reveals significant headroom in handling interdependent engineering constraints. △ Less

    Conformal Annotation with Randomized Expert Review: Application to Online Cell-Type Labeling

    Authors: Xuhe Zhang, Zhi Huang, Ying Jin

    Abstract: …while suggesting candidate labels for the others for downstream resolution. We prove that this workflow provides long-run control of cumulative error over both the full stream and the cells outside of the audit sample, without stationarity or exchangeability assumptions. We further enhance the framework by introducing class-conditional guarantees to ensure… ▽ More Artificial intelligence (AI) models can accelerate cell-type annotation in digital pathology, but safely scaling their use requires determining, with error control, which model-generated labels can be accepted without expert review. This is challenging in evolving workflows because cell morphology and type composition may change across tissue regions and slides, making one-time calibration unreliable. We propose Conformal Annotation with Randomized Expert review under Distribution shift (CARED), a distribution-free framework that leverages randomized expert audit to achieve long-run error control for AI-assisted annotation under distribution shift. For each incoming batch, CARED uniformly selects a subset of cases for expert review and uses the acquired labels to calibrate later decisions. Among unreviewed cases, CARED determines a subset of them to safely skip expert labeling, while suggesting candidate labels for the others for downstream resolution. We prove that this workflow provides long-run control of cumulative error over both the full stream and the cells outside of the audit sample, without stationarity or exchangeability assumptions. We further enhance the framework by introducing class-conditional guarantees to ensure error control within important but rare cell types, together with a utility module to target more automatic resolutions, smaller candidate sets, and greater biological coherence, without compromising validity. Experiments on pancreas, lung, and ovary annotation streams, together with a stream constructed from real expert annotations, show that CARED controls the cumulative errors while a substantial fraction of cells (up to 70%) bypass expert review. △ Less

    Video Generation Meets Agent Harness: Agentic Video Generation, Agentic World Modeling and Recursive Self-Improvement

    Authors: Liaoyuan Fan, Yuhao Zheng, Dongxing Mao, Hangyu Ran, Siyuan Hu, Weijia Mao, Rui Zhao, Weijia Wu, Guian Fang, Yanzhe Chen, Zeyu Zhu, Yuxin Jiang, Yiren Song, Yuchao Gu, Junchao Yi, Qi Hu, Dan Kong, Alex Jinpeng Wang, Kevin Qinghong Lin, Mike Zheng Shou, Wei Liu

    Abstract: …We identify how these improvements feed back into subsequent development. Across these directions, we analyze essential agent capabilities, including audiovisual perception, long-horizon planning, memory, code-based interaction, feedback-guided control, skill reuse, and multi-agent coordination. We conclude with chall… ▽ More Video generation is shifting from end-to-end synthesis toward agentic generation, where planning, tool use, and feedback enable coherent, controllable multi-shot videos. In parallel, video world models provide action-conditioned observations and predicted futures, serving as interactive environments for agents. We study their intersection through the agent harness: an executable runtime that constructs context, exposes tools, maintains state, routes feedback, and manages budgets around video models. This report examines three directions. First, agentic video generation, where harnesses coordinate scripting, shot planning, asset management, production, and editing, alongside the evaluation challenges of these systems. Second, agentic world modeling, where harnesses mediate observation, control, execution, and state. We discuss hybrid designs in which game engines or agent-written programs maintain world state while video models render observations, and how agents and world models can mutually evaluate and improve one another. Third, recursive self-improvement (RSI) in video model development, distinguishing output revisions, updates to harness state, generator parameters, and evaluators, and changes to the improvement procedure itself. We identify how these improvements feed back into subsequent development. Across these directions, we analyze essential agent capabilities, including audiovisual perception, long-horizon planning, memory, code-based interaction, feedback-guided control, skill reuse, and multi-agent coordination. We conclude with challenges in autonomous video production, lifelong memory, programmable hybrid control, autonomous model development, provenance, cost accounting, and evaluation linking video quality and task performance to sustained improvements in models and harnesses. △ Less

    cs.CV

    Hierarchical Step Decomposition for Training-free Online Video Step Grounding

    Authors: Long Zhou, Luca Zanella, Paolo Rota, Massimiliano Mancini, Elisa Ricci

    Abstract: …understanding capabilities of Large Multimodal Models. HSD addresses two central challenges in online grounding: constructing semantically coherent temporal segments from a streaming video, and bridging the semantic gap between coarse procedural step descriptions and visual observations. To construct elementary temporal units, HSD incrementally groups incomi… ▽ More Given a set of procedural steps, Video Step Grounding (VSG) aims to identify which steps occur in a video and localize when they are performed. Existing methods are either offline, requiring access to the full video before making predictions, or online, but relying on fixed-length video segments producing noisy, over-segmented outputs. In this work, we propose Hierarchical Step Decomposition (HSD), a training-free framework for online VSG that leverages the semantic understanding capabilities of Large Multimodal Models. HSD addresses two central challenges in online grounding: constructing semantically coherent temporal segments from a streaming video, and bridging the semantic gap between coarse procedural step descriptions and visual observations. To construct elementary temporal units, HSD incrementally groups incoming segments into semantically consistent events, using an anchor caption that is continuously validated against the visual stream. Based on these events, we perform zero-shot procedural step classification and selectively refine uncertain predictions by decomposing candidate steps into finegrained substeps that capture more nuanced visual evidence. By operating on semantically coherent events rather than every fixed-length segment, HSD reduces redundant computation and produces more stable predictions. Experiments on HT-Step, Ego4D Goal-Step, and CrossTask show that HSD substantially outperforms state-of-the-art methods, overall achieving better step recognition, more accurate temporal localization, and faster inference. △ Less

  17. arXiv:2610.40130  [pdf, ps, other] 

    cs.LG cs.AI

    Escaping Path Mirages in Offline Goal-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning

    Authors: Junhyeon Bae, Jaebak Hwang, Gwanwoo Choi, Minung Kim, Seungyul Han

    Abstract: Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning remains challenging in stochastic long-… ▽ More Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning remains challenging in stochastic long-horizon settings, where compounding value estimation errors hinder reliable goal reaching. While prior methods have sought to address this challenge through various approaches, a key challenge remains path mirage, where agents overcommit to spuriously successful trajectories in offline datasets induced by stochasticity, often leading to failure on difficult tasks. To address this issue, we propose Branch-aware Graph Planning (BGP), which captures stochastic branching structures by identifying high-variability points as graph nodes and designing goal-conditioned edges with segmented learning to avoid unreliable branches and reduce unnecessary stochasticity along paths. As a result, BGP enables reliable goal reaching even in highly stochastic long-horizon environments, and experiments on diverse OGBench tasks show that it substantially outperforms prior state-of-the-art offline goal-conditioned RL methods. Our implementation is available at https://umlic.github.io/papers/BGP/. △ Less

    A heuristic for optimal coordination of autonomous vehicles: dynamic problems

    Authors: Saugat Shahi, Filippo Fabiani, Mario Zanon

    Abstract: …of the current crossing order, and a memory of evaluated swaps that persists across replanning steps avoids redundant computations. Simulations of traffic scenarios involving streams of a few hundred heterogeneous vehicles show that the proposed scheme recovers most of the optimality gap incurred by first-in-first-out scheduling. ▽ More The coordination of autonomous vehicles at unsignalized intersections requires deciding both the trajectories of the vehicles and their crossing order, with the additional difficulty that the set of vehicles to be coordinated changes as traffic flows through the intersection. Since the resulting mixed-integer optimal control problem is hard to solve online, we propose an arrival-triggered receding-horizon scheme built around a schedule-refinement heuristic: for a fixed crossing order, a single nonlinear program (NLP) optimizes all trajectories and crossing times, and the first-order sensitivities of the cost with respect to the vehicle schedule are used to select adjacent swaps that are likely to reduce the overall cost. Replanning is triggered only when a new vehicle arrives, the new vehicle is appended at the tail of the current crossing order, and a memory of evaluated swaps that persists across replanning steps avoids redundant computations. Simulations of traffic scenarios involving streams of a few hundred heterogeneous vehicles show that the proposed scheme recovers most of the optimality gap incurred by first-in-first-out scheduling. △ Less

    cs.AI

    CauProphet: Teaching Agents to Develop Causal Cognition like Humans

    Authors: Kaiwen Wu, Chaochao Lu

    Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly support long-horizon tasks by leveraging external memory and past experience. However, existing agents mainly retrieve records or summaries, lacking the ability to form and refine causal understanding of the world. Inspired by human causal cognition, we introduce CauProphe… ▽ More Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly support long-horizon tasks by leveraging external memory and past experience. However, existing agents mainly retrieve records or summaries, lacking the ability to form and refine causal understanding of the world. Inspired by human causal cognition, we introduce CauProphet, an agent that develops and updates causal knowledge within its Causal Cognition. For each task, CauProphet combines relevant knowledge in Causal Cognition with current evidence to construct a Causal Belief, a structured belief capturing causal factors and the effects of possible actions. It uses this belief for decision-making and continuously updates both the belief and Causal Cognition as new observations emerge, enabling causal knowledge that persists beyond individual experiences. Evaluations demonstrate that CauProphet progressively acquires causal knowledge and transfers it to future decision-making. In financial research, CauProphet leverages prior discoveries to improve downstream analyses and outperforms all baselines in mean rank correlation with next-day returns, achieving a 25.4% improvement over a baseline agent. It also improves prediction accuracy from 21.3% to 28.6% in dynamic real-world settings. Further experiments validate its ability to perform reliable causal analysis and action selection across scientific and everyday tasks, demonstrating the potential of causal experience learning for next-generation LLM agents. The project repository is available at https://github.com/OpenCausaLab/CauProphet. △ Less

    LOGIV: Graph-Verified Execution with Causal Local Repair for Long-Horizon Robot Tasks

    Authors: Enqiao Lu, Yuxiang Xie, Xingrui Yu, Yuyang Wang, Yipeng Gu, Zhenglin Wan, Pengfei Zhou, Wangbo Zhao, Yang You, Yuxiang Gao, Ivor Tsang

    Abstract: Agentic robots must turn a high-level goal into actions while keeping the physical conditions needed for future steps valid. A completed action does not ensure that its effects will persist: a later disturbance can invalidate support that the remaining plan still assumes. We present LOGIV, a graph engineering framework for agentic robot execution that uses an explicit task graph to connect symboli… ▽ More Agentic robots must turn a high-level goal into actions while keeping the physical conditions needed for future steps valid. A completed action does not ensure that its effects will persist: a later disturbance can invalidate support that the remaining plan still assumes. We present LOGIV, a graph engineering framework for agentic robot execution that uses an explicit task graph to connect symbolic planning, observation-based verification, and embodied action. LOGIV compiles a validated plan into condition-support and resource-ordering edges, checks their physical validity during execution, and uses dependency tracing to determine what must be restored after a failure. Repair is constrained by retained unfinished actions, and the combined continuation is symbolically validated before execution resumes. The framework operates above existing Vision-Language-Action (VLA) and World Action Model (WAM) policies without adding trainable components. Across 20 dual-arm tasks from RoboTwin~V2 and BiCoord with three execution backbones, LOGIV improves mean task success without injected disturbances by 10.67 and 15.33 percentage points, respectively. Under predefined perturbations, it achieves higher task completion and condition restoration than three adapted recovery baselines for every backbone on both benchmarks. On three real-robot tasks, successful trials increase from 12/30 to 17/30. These results support explicit, continuously checked task dependencies as a practical interface for reliable agentic robot execution. △ Less

    NORmal: Architecting 3D Ferroelectric NOR Flash as Main Memory -- From Co-Fabricated BEOL Arrays to 200B-Class Multi-Agent LLMs in a Smartphone

    Abstract: …a team on the device requires 200B-class models and contexts that exceed phone DRAM many times over, yet existing tiers treat flash as storage: UFS starves per-token weight streaming, and mobile High-Bandwidth Flash (HBF) needs a redesigned SoC and cannot absorb continuous KV writes. Running such a team on the device requires 200B-class models and contexts that exceed phone DRAM many times over, yet existing tiers treat flash as storage: UFS starves per-token weight streaming, and mobile High-Bandwidth Flash (HBF) needs a redesigned SoC and cannot absorb continuous KV writes. We show that a non-volatile memory can join an unmodified LPDDR6 bus as a plain rank only if its access latency fits the DRAM timing window, a condition no NAND tier meets. We present NORmal, to our knowledge the first main-memory architecture for 3D ferroelectric NOR flash. We have fabricated and experimentally characterized back-end-of-line (BEOL) 3D FeNOR arrays that co-integrate dense 3-bit MLC and fast, high-endurance in-place-writable SLC with sub-7.5 ns sensing. NORmal turns these co-fabricated arrays into a byte-addressable, deterministic-timing load/store rank on an unmodified LPDDR6 interface through an under-array DRAM-emulation bridge, staggered bank reads with zero-cycle weight swizzling, and posted SLC writes. Across this rank and DRAM, NORmal places agent state by mutability and lifetime rather than by hotness, serving 200B-class models and persistent planner contexts in place while dynamically partitioning subchannels across agent phases. In serving-level simulation on production agent traces, NORmal is 8.9$\times$--28.0$\times$ faster than UFS offloading, within 0.4% of an unlimited-DRAM ideal, and 2.8$\times$--6.3$\times$ faster than HBF on sessions beyond 256K tokens, with 8.1$\times$--17.8$\times$ less memory energy per token. △ Less

    Randomized Symplectic Model Reduction: One-Pass Basis Construction and Sketched Hamiltonian Assembly

    Authors: Robin Herkert, Bernard Haasdonk, Jörg Fehr

    Abstract: …reduced-operator assembly can involve substantial offline costs. Randomized numerical linear algebra can reduce these costs through low-dimensional sketches while supporting streaming and parallel implementations. We develop randomized techniques for two parts of the offline stage while preserving the Hamiltonian structure of the reduced dynamics. First, we… ▽ More Symplectic model order reduction of Hamiltonian systems yields reduced dynamics that conserve the reduced Hamiltonian along exact trajectories, avoiding the artificial energy drift that can arise from conventional projection methods. For large-scale spatial discretizations, however, basis construction and reduced-operator assembly can involve substantial offline costs. Randomized numerical linear algebra can reduce these costs through low-dimensional sketches while supporting streaming and parallel implementations. We develop randomized techniques for two parts of the offline stage while preserving the Hamiltonian structure of the reduced dynamics. First, we introduce the one-pass sketched randomized cSVD (Ops-rcSVD), a one-pass randomized variant of the complex-SVD algorithm. Combining randomized range finding with an independent left sketch, Ops-rcSVD constructs an ortho-symplectic reduced basis from a single pass over the snapshot ensemble. Second, for quadratic Hamiltonian systems, we introduce a randomized reduced-Hamiltonian assembly based on sketching the reduced basis and operator actions, yielding a reduced Hamiltonian system by construction. We derive bounds for the snapshot projection error, uniform-in-parameter perturbation bounds for the sketched reduced Hamiltonian, and an energy-norm estimate for the difference between reduced and sketched reduced trajectories. Experiments on large-scale systems show that Ops-rcSVD attains projection accuracy comparable to deterministic and two-pass randomized constructions while reducing basis-construction time. They further demonstrate accurate sketched reduced-Hamiltonian assembly when storing the complete snapshot ensemble is no longer feasible. △ Less

    Surprise Alone Learns Lies: Evidence-Gated Continual Learning for LLMs from Streaming Claims

    Authors: Abdelkader Metales, Miriam Grabenheinrich

    Abstract: …learning methods for large language models increasingly decide what to learn by surprise, keeping the inputs the model finds least predictable. This rule is unsafe when the stream contains lies. A planted falsehood about something the model knows has low probability under the model's belief, so it is highly surprising by construction, and a surprise-driv… ▽ More Continual learning methods for large language models increasingly decide what to learn by surprise, keeping the inputs the model finds least predictable. This rule is unsafe when the stream contains lies. A planted falsehood about something the model knows has low probability under the model's belief, so it is highly surprising by construction, and a surprise-driven learner absorbs nearly every such lie, at a rate similar to random selection with the same budget. We study the problem with LoRA-adapted students (Qwen2.5-1.5B, Qwen2.5-3B and Llama-3.2-3B) that learn from a stream of true and false claims about invented people and real CounterFact facts. We report four findings. (1) A verifier key, a judge that must approve each claim, removes lies to the extent that its verifier is accurate; real LLM judges are cautious and reject unsourced truths and even source-backed updates. (2) Calibrated regression tests on unrelated knowledge slow learning down without filtering out lies. (3) The original model's own prior flags claims that contradict what it knows, without a judge. It separates lies about known facts from true new facts (AUC 0.78-0.83 across three models) but not from legitimate updates of the same kind of fact (AUC 0.53). It saves 43% of judge calls, but an attacker with access to the model can choose lies that pass it. (4) If trusted sources exist that an attacker cannot plant, routing contradicted claims to evidence recovers legitimate updates and the combined system has the lowest poison rate among learned claims; when an attacker plants matching sources for 30% of the lies, the system ends up worse than the judge alone. The acceptance signals answer different questions, and none of them decides on its own whether a claim is true. △ Less

    GROME: GROunding Factorized MEmory into Video World Models

    Authors: Fang Yu, Xiaoxue Wu, Haiyu Zhang, Yangfu Li, Botong Zhao, Yu Qiao, Tianfan Xue, Yue Lu, Xinyuan Chen

    Abstract: Long-… ▽ More Long-horizon consistency is essential for video world models, yet it becomes difficult to maintain once observed content falls outside the context window. Existing memory mechanisms either retrieve history as implicit context, which remains constrained by context capacity and entangles static and dynamic information or consolidate history into explicit global representations, which struggle to model evolving dynamics and accumulate geometric errors. To overcome these limitations, we introduce \textbf{GROME}, a novel factorized memory framework that augments video world models for long-horizon coherence across both static scenes and dynamic entities. Experiments on long-horizon revisit scenarios demonstrate that GROME substantially enhances scene persistence and dynamic entity fidelity over existing methods, all while preserving the visual quality and controllability of the frozen backbone without additional training.     Caerus: Prefetching by Thinking Globally and Acting Locally

    Authors: Jacky Wong, Jacob Alexandrou, Yinting Huang, Luke Panayi, Lev Mukhanov, Sam Ainsworth, Paul Kelly

    Abstract: …prefetching, prefetches are issued at an address delta relative to the most recently observed access, selecting deltas that have exhibited high coverage in the recent access-stream history. The Best-Offset Prefetcher (BOP) exemplifies this approach by selecting a single uniformly-issued global offset that delivers timely prefetches. However, its lack of cont… ▽ More Modern processors rely heavily on data prefetching to bridge the growing performance gap between computation and memory accesses. In offset prefetching, prefetches are issued at an address delta relative to the most recently observed access, selecting deltas that have exhibited high coverage in the recent access-stream history. The Best-Offset Prefetcher (BOP) exemplifies this approach by selecting a single uniformly-issued global offset that delivers timely prefetches. However, its lack of context-sensitive accuracy tracking results in significantly increased DRAM traffic and sometimes disappointing performance. Conversely, Berti, the state-of-the-art offset prefetcher, achieves impressive prefetching performance and accuracy. However, its Program-Counter (PC)-local approach to extracting offsets limits its ability to capture global memory patterns, meaning there are many scenarios where simpler global offset prefetchers, such as BOP, outperform it. Observing the limitations of contemporary offset prefetchers, we present Caerus: an offset prefetcher that employs PC-local filtering on multiple non-overlapping globally derived offsets. Caerus extracts multiple offsets from the global memory trace and maximizes their combined coverage by training each offset only on accesses not already covered by previously learned offsets. The accuracy of each extracted offset is tracked per-PC based on whether it can cover future accesses for any PC. This allows Caerus to address complex delta patterns while maintaining high accuracy. In SPECspeed 2017, Caerus gains a geomean speedup of 8.3%, outperforming Berti by 2.0% while achieving low hardware and DRAM-traffic overhead. △ Less

    Authors: Kiljoon Han, Kyumin Hwang, Wonheok Choi, Jeonghoon Kim, Sunghoom Im

    Abstract: …accuracy, but their rigid, fixed update schedules incur heavy computational costs and a strict trade-off between speed and accuracy. To address this, we propose \textbf{DEQ-Stereo}, a novel video stereo… ▽ More Iterative refinement models have significantly improved stereo matching accuracy, but their rigid, fixed update schedules incur heavy computational costs and a strict trade-off between speed and accuracy. This challenge is magnified in video streams, where traditional online methods resort to reducing feature channels or relying on expensive temporal warping. To address this, we propose \textbf{DEQ-Stereo}, a novel video stereo matching framework based on the Deep Equilibrium (DEQ) paradigm. Instead of unrolling a fixed number of iterations, DEQ-Stereo reformulates the refinement as an implicit fixed-point problem, adaptively terminating once convergence is met. To ensure fast and stable intra-frame convergence, we introduce the \textbf{Convolutional Simplified Gated Unit (ConvSGU)}, which eliminates unnecessary non-linearities by modeling the latent state as a gated, element-wise sum of inputs. Furthermore, to exploit cross-frame temporal coherence, we propose \textbf{Temporal State Transfer (TST)}, a warm-start strategy that propagates the converged hidden state to the next frame without requiring explicit pose or warping priors. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that DEQ-Stereo delivers state-of-the-art performance with a superior accuracy-to-latency trade-off. △ Less

    FutureWorld: A Plug-and-Play Causal Next-State Predictor for Interactive Video World Exploration

    Authors: Guangyuan Li, Kelu Yao, Shuolin Xu, Xinlei Yu, Wei Xing, Lei Zhao, Peng-Tao Jiang, Xiaogang Xu, Xiaobin Hu

    Abstract: …across three benchmarks for interactive long-video generation and action-conditioned world modeling. The project page is at https://GuangYuanKK.github.io/futureworld. ▽ More Interactive video world models aim to follow user actions while maintaining scene consistency over extended interactions. History-based methods reuse generated latents to preserve scene content, but three limitations of this reuse contribute to error recirculation. 1) The latent generated at each interaction is retained as history, potentially containing blurred details, geometric distortions, or semantic drift. 2) Although retrieved historical content matches the current scene, it can still contain errors that are carried into the next chunk. 3) New outputs then become history for later interactions, potentially reinforcing the same errors through repeated reuse. To limit this cycle, we aim to predict the future scene state from the current generated latent and the next action before generating the next interaction's video chunk. This future-state prior provides guidance for the next chunk, with the aim of reducing the errors carried forward from history. To this end, we propose a plug-and-play Causal Next-Chunk Predictor (C-NCP) with cross-model adaptability and prediction-guided modulation. We integrate C-NCP into four base generators and evaluate the augmented models across three benchmarks for interactive long-video generation and action-conditioned world modeling. Extensive experiments demonstrate reduced error accumulation and improved long-horizon consistency across diverse generation settings. The project page is at https://GuangYuanKK.github.io/futureworld. △ Less

    The Cost of Working Memory in Streaming Mean Estimation

    Authors: Zeyu Wu, Zihao Wang, Minghua Deng

    Abstract: A streaming estimator must compute from the evidence that fits in memory. We determine when storing an accurate mean is easier than computing it. For Gaussian mean estimation over the Euclidean unit ball in $\mathbb{R}^d$, we establish the minimax rate under squared error with $K$ persistent states, a known horizon, and a public time index:… ▽ More A streaming estimator must compute from the evidence that fits in memory. We determine when storing an accurate mean is easier than computing it. For Gaussian mean estimation over the Euclidean unit ball in $\mathbb{R}^d$, we establish the minimax rate under squared error with $K$ persistent states, a known horizon, and a public time index: $\min\{1, K^{-2/d}+\frac{d}{n} (1+\frac{\log(ed)}{1+(\log K)/d})\}$. Unrestricted computation followed by $K$-level encoding has rate $\min\{1,d/n+K^{-2/d}\}$. At a fixed sufficiently small error, statistically optimal sample size therefore requires order $d\log d$ working bits, while order $d$ bits suffice to store the answer. The lower bound tracks repeated state compression through an entropy potential obtained by Gaussian smoothing. A matching construction combines a multiscale modular counter with workspace reused across coordinate groups. In one dimension, it attains the terminal rate $n^{-1}+K^{-2}$. Any fixed confidence requirement that the realized loss not exceed the zero baseline preserves these rates. Increasing confidence creates a further memory requirement: at fixed nontrivial risk in every fixed dimension, failure probability $e^{-γn}$ requires and permits order $\log n$ working states for sufficiently small fixed $γ>0$. In the scalar problem, a fixed terminal alphabet suffices under the same protection. These bounds separate the memory needed to represent an answer from the memory needed to accumulate evidence and validate a decision. △ Less

A single adversarial objective then suffices: in one step, ROAD reaches 11.03 FID on SDXL and 13.73 on SD3.5-M on COCO-10K, surpassing the multi-step teachers at a fraction of distribution matching's training compute. △ Less

    Authors: Yihua Pan, Jiaqing Kou

    Abstract: …jet. These… ▽ More Causal analysis characterizes directional dependencies in dynamical systems, while such information is not typically used to inform reduced-order modeling of unsteady flows. This study proposes a Granger Causality-informed Dynamic Mode Decomposition (GC-DMD) framework that incorporates such information into data-driven modeling and modal analysis of high-dimensional flow data. The framework uses conditional Granger causality estimates to regularize the identification of the DMD operator, preferentially suppressing weakly supported interactions while preserving dynamically relevant couplings. This regularization promotes sparse operators informed by directional predictive dependencies, leading to improved predictive accuracy and identification of dynamically relevant modes. GC-DMD is evaluated on a linear system with a known coupling structure, lid-driven cavity flows with varying dynamical complexity, and a broadband transonic turbulent jet. Compared with standard DMD, GC-DMD improves operator identification accuracy in the linear benchmark and improves the robustness of the identified spectrum while reducing long-horizon prediction errors in the cavity flows. For the turbulent jet, it yields a sparser operator with improved prediction accuracy. These results demonstrate the potential of incorporating causality information to guide the identification of sparse, predictive reduced-order models of complex flows. However, ML predictors couple short histories with fixed horizons, fitting the training trace's node layout rather than learning transferable graph patterns. We propose LoCoMAP, a long-context representation-learning framework for memory-access prediction with independently controlled observation history and prediction horizon. We evaluate Connected Components (CC), PageRank (PR), and Single-Source Shortest Paths (SSSP) on six synthetic graph families, with Erdos-Renyi (ER) and Grid as random and deterministic controls. We test two interventions: same-family regeneration changes connectivity, while node shuffling disrupts layout but preserves connectivity. Across 18 graph-workload pairs, extending history from 8 to 128 accesses improves F1 by 7.8%, while extending prediction horizon from 8 to 512 improves F1 by 88.3%. For CC, regeneration is less disruptive than shuffling: without retraining, long context retains 95.4% of original F1 and outperforms short context in six families. Under full shuffle, long context improves CC F1 by 33.6% across ER, Barabasi-Albert, Copying Model, and Stochastic Block Model (SBM), with a 2.06x gain on SBM, but not on Watts-Strogatz or Grid, where near-zero delta mass shifts into heavy tails beyond the predictor's range. Long context outperforms short context on six real-world CC graphs. At full shuffle, long-context PR retains 57.6% of original F1 and averages 11.0% above short context. Both SSSP models retain about 30% of original F1 under full shuffle as distance updates access vertex data in a new order. Within these bounds, long-context predictors are robust across related graphs and layouts without retraining, enabling a generalizable graph prefetcher. △ Less

    The Price of Scale in Proactive Memory Recall

    Authors: Yichuan Chen, Xin Wang

    Abstract: …at every turn whether anything in its store is worth an interruption. We show that this decision is a multiple-testing problem whose difficulty grows with the store size N. On streaming replays of LongMemEval, a similarity threshold tuned to 5% false alarms at N=8 reaches 84-100% at N=2048 across embedding models and cue types, and LoCoMo and LLM judges show… ▽ More A long-lived assistant that brings up memories without being asked must decide at every turn whether anything in its store is worth an interruption. We show that this decision is a multiple-testing problem whose difficulty grows with the store size N. On streaming replays of LongMemEval, a similarity threshold tuned to 5% false alarms at N=8 reaches 84-100% at N=2048 across embedding models and cue types, and LoCoMo and LLM judges show it too. We prove that a gate which thresholds the best of N independent irrelevant scores at false-alarm rate alpha operates at a per-item false-positive rate of about -ln(1-alpha)/N, which pins its hit rate to the far left of the single-comparison ROC curve and, under regular variation, makes it regularly varying in N. A finite-pool form of this bound predicts the hit rate of a label-tuned threshold on held-out questions to within 6.1 points. At matched false alarms the deployed score recalls 14-26 points more than raw cosine. A cue-swap conformal gate, which ranks the current cue among other cues on the same store, has a finite-sample false-alarm guarantee under exchangeability. With a background drawn from the user's unfiltered history it keeps false alarms at 3-6% on LongMemEval at the hit rate of a label-tuned threshold; on LoCoMo it overshoots or turns conservative depending on where its reference cues come from. In a chronological replay with the user's own earlier messages as references, it speaks on 6-8% of session-opening turns, against 57% for the deployed gate at N=2048, at about 50 ms per decision on one CPU thread. A cross-encoder reranker pays the same price of scale, and on a prospective-memory benchmark, similarity gates recall at most 9% of constraints at controlled false alarms. One human and two LLM annotators judge most false alarms unhelpful for a response. Code and data: https://github.com/RiverChenStillCompiling/price-of-scale △ Less

    Emergence of an acoustic black hole in a lattice model

    Authors: Antonino Flachi

    Abstract: We build an acoustic black hole in a lattice model: a draining vortex evolved by the local collide-and-stream rule of a lattice-Boltzmann model, with no acoustic metric imposed, the geometry being measured from the steady state. A horizon emerges, first located from the measured Mach profile and then confirmed by phonon ray tracing on the measured metric, an… ▽ More We build an acoustic black hole in a lattice model: a draining vortex evolved by the local collide-and-stream rule of a lattice-Boltzmann model, with no acoustic metric imposed, the geometry being measured from the steady state. A horizon emerges, first located from the measured Mach profile and then confirmed by phonon ray tracing on the measured metric, and the ringdown after a perturbation follows approximately the quasinormal frequencies of the draining bathtub. The residual departures are traced to the cell-scale structure of the horizon, which is not an ideal absorber. We model it in two ways: as a reflective horizon on the ideal bathtub, with two free parameters (the modulus and phase of the reflectivity), and as the sonic point of the realised flow absorbing in its own frame, with no adjustable parameter. The second describes the horizon and the non-rotating spectrum best. Superradiance has two ingredients: the emergent geometry, which amplifies in a loss-free calculation on the measured metric, and the horizon, whose absorption frame sets the width and strength of the amplified band but leaves no trace in the ringdown. Both models are used to examine superradiance, which has not been observed in the present toy model: whether the lattice hole superradiates dynamically remains open. The lattice leaves its own imprint as well: a fourfold anisotropy of the sonic surface and a surface gravity set by the cell-scale structure of the horizon. △ Less

Large language model (LLM) agents can now rewrite these recipes, enabling recursive self-improvement (RSI) of recommender systems. Existing agents propose one edit per round, fully retrain the recommender, and keep the edit only if its ranking quality improves. Unlike programs or patches that can be scored by execution, a recommender edit changes how the model is trained, which raises two challenges. First, ranking feedback is delayed: an edit's ranking quality is known only after full retraining, so much of the retraining budget goes to edits that are later discarded. Second, the signals available before retraining are unreliable: sampled training losses can look solved while full-ranking performance remains poor, and they are not comparable across edits that change the loss or sampler. We propose RecRSI, which generates diverse candidate edits from a diagnosis of the current model and selects one for full retraining using early ranking feedback: each candidate receives a short trial, the first steps of its full training run, scored by full-ranking NDCG@10 on held-out probe users. With two sequential recommenders on three datasets and the same number of full retrainings, RecRSI significantly outperforms existing self-evolving agents in every setting, and on both Amazon datasets reaches in its first round a ranking quality that none of them reaches in five. △ Less

    Submitted 29 October, 2026; originally announced October 2026.

Inspired by human book transcription via localized focus without re-scanning preceding pages, we introduce Unlimited OCR 2, a document parsing model that looks only at what it transcribes. Building on Unlimited OCR, we upgrade all the decoder attention modules to Sparse Reference Sliding Window Attention (SR-SWA), bounding active reference tokens to a fixed capacity and thus sustaining constant computational costs across arbitrarily long documents. By combining SR-SWA with Unlimited-Horizon Decoding Paradigm, Unlimited OCR 2 enables document parsing over virtually unlimited lengths without prohibitive memory overhead. At the hundred-page scale, Unlimited OCR 2 achieves 7.0$\times$ and 5.6$\times$ per-step speedups over Full Attention and R-SWA, respectively. Furthermore, it delivers state-of-the-art end-to-end performance on both MPDocBench and MarathonDocBench, our newly proposed benchmark targeting hundred-page document parsing. Ultimately, the capacity for uninterrupted, one-shot document parsing signifies a fundamental paradigm shift into the OCR 3.0 era. △ Less

    PFA: Attention Can Be Progressively Focused in Long-Sequence Generative Recommendation

    Authors: Pengjie Zhou, Weiqin Yang, Haibo Xing, Hao Deng, Jinxin Hu

    Abstract: …recommendation (GR), but computing full attention over long histories during autoregressive beam search decoding incurs substantial online inference latency. To accelerate long-… ▽ More Long user behavioral sequences improve the performance of generative recommendation (GR), but computing full attention over long histories during autoregressive beam search decoding incurs substantial online inference latency. To accelerate long-sequence decoding, existing approaches typically rely on static history compression or dynamic top-k sparse attention; however, the former risks losing critical semantics, whereas the latter incurs significant overhead from repeated token selection and non-contiguous memory access. In this work, we thoroughly analyze attention patterns during GR decoding and reveal two key properties: (i) progressive attention focusing, where attention progressively focuses on a small subset of critical historical tokens; and (ii) cross-step ranking consistency, where their importance rankings remain relatively stable across subsequent decoding steps. Motivated by these observations, we propose Progressively Focused Attention (PFA), an efficient sparse decoding framework for long-sequence GR. PFA retains full attention at the initial decoding step to explore global historical preferences. It then establishes a shared importance ranking through cross-beam weighted aggregation and reorders the key-value cache of the selected tokens into a contiguous memory layout. Subsequent decoding steps reuse this ranking and geometrically reduce the token budget, preserving critical semantics while lowering computational and memory access overhead. Extensive experiments on two public benchmarks and a large-scale industrial dataset demonstrate that PFA outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. A 7-day online A/B test on a large-scale e-commerce advertising platform further confirms that PFA delivers substantial business gains while maintaining low inference latency. Our code is publicly available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/PFA-review-088D. △ Less

    Goal-Aware Dynamic Multi-Agent Collaboration for Long-Horizon Tasks

    Authors: Xu Shen, Lingyong Yan, Shuaiqiang Wang, Jianmin Wu, Zhikai Chen, Jiliang Tang, Jie Ren

    Abstract: LLM-based agents are increasingly used to complete complex, long-… ▽ More LLM-based agents are increasingly used to complete complex, long-horizon tasks without human intervention. To make progress toward the task goal, agents need to perform different duties, such as planning and verification, when needed. To understand how single agents manage these duties during long-horizon execution, we conduct a preliminary study. Our analysis shows that verification has the strongest association with task success, but often occurs late, leaving limited opportunities for correction. We also find that the proportion of duty-relevant information decreases as records from different duties accumulate in a shared context. These findings lead us to examine whether multi-agent systems (MAS) can address these problems by assigning duties to agents with separate contexts. Our analysis shows that this separation alone is insufficient. Agents can lose track of the overall goal and repeat prior investigations. They may also lack necessary background while receiving irrelevant information from others. To maintain goal awareness and coordinate collaboration as task needs change, we propose Goal-Aware Dynamic Multi-Agent Collaboration (GAD). It uses a growing execution graph to organize subtasks and their dependencies. To keep agents focused on their goals, each context retains the original instruction and the assigned task's goal and acceptance conditions. To adapt collaboration and information sharing during execution, agents can divide tasks into subtasks and specify dependencies based on execution evidence. These dependencies guide when agents work and which results they receive. Verification and recovery check subtask results and full task completion against their goals, providing feedback for repair when checks fail. △ Less

    Authors: Renzhi Wu, Li Sheng, Zikun Cui, Junjie Yang, Tai Guo, Keke Zhai, Yan Li, Jerry Fu, Nipun Mathur, Sri Reddy, Hong Li

    Abstract: Long-sequence recommendation can improve along two axes: \emph{evidence scaling} exposes longer histories, while \emph{reasoning scaling} adds layers that interpret that evidence for a candidate. Existing models couple them: sequence encoders propagate every added event through depth, whereas one-shot selection prevents later layers from revisiting omitted evidence. We introduce the Latent Workspace Ranker (LWR), which keeps typed evidence in persistent banks and propagates a fixed-size candidate-local workspace. Each layer rereads the banks from its evolving workspace and archives compact post-interaction state, allowing history length and reasoning depth to grow independently. We compare four architectures across various history--depth configurations using evidence scaling at fixed depth, reasoning scaling at fixed 10K history, and their joint compute-efficient frontier. LWR leads all families at long histories, continues improving with reasoning compute, and converts additional training compute into quality gains at the fastest fitted rate on the joint frontier. At 10K history, it improves NE by 0.19% over HSTU while using 69.6% fewer forward GFLOP/request. It also outperforms existing baselines on two billion-scale ranking surfaces. These results show that separating addressable evidence from propagated state makes reading more and reasoning deeper independently controllable and jointly useful. △ Less

    cs.LG

    Closed Gates Leak: Decomposing Adjacent-Layer Cancellation in Gated Language Models

    Authors: Harvey Dam, Ganesh Gopalakrishnan

    Abstract: In transformer language models with gated multilayer perceptrons (MLPs), adjacent layers write partly against each other into the residual stream. Previous work interpreted this cancellation as layers proposing features and the next layer selectively rejecting the inappropriate ones. We decompose the cancellation by removing the previous layer's write fr… ▽ More In transformer language models with gated multilayer perceptrons (MLPs), adjacent layers write partly against each other into the residual stream. Previous work interpreted this cancellation as layers proposing features and the next layer selectively rejecting the inappropriate ones. We decompose the cancellation by removing the previous layer's write from the input of the next MLP: what the next layer writes along that write splits into what it writes anyway and how much less its MLP writes because the write is present, which we call the damping, and we trace the damping to the neurons that carry it. In gated MLPs the damping is carried by neurons whose gates are closed. SiLU and GELU are slightly negative below zero, so a closed gate still passes a small negative multiple of what the neuron reads, and a neuron whose output weight points along its input weight then subtracts a fraction of the direction it read. Summed over such neurons, closed gates contract the average direction a gated MLP reads, and the previous layer's write is not singled out: the same layer's write at another token is damped as much or more, and the eigenvalue signature from which that work concluded that the correction is selective appears for another token's write as well. Over training the damping grows while what the next layer writes anyway stays flat, and cancellation begins when the damping overtakes it; annealing the negative side of the gate activation away in continued pretraining removes the damping and the cancellation. Adjacent-layer cancellation is therefore weak evidence about which features a model rejects: it is produced by a contraction that gated MLPs apply, on average over directions, to whatever they read. △ Less

They were likely born in situ, although the high orbital eccentricity of BH 140 and its mild deficiency in α-elements leave other possibilities open. Although undoubtedly accreted, BLISS 1 does not exhibit a clear dynamical association with any known progenitor, lying in a high energy (E) - high vertical angular momentum (LZ ) prograde orbit reaching a maximum height from the Galactic plane of zmax ~ 8 kpc. Its chemical properties seem to favor an in situ origin, which might be connected with the Aleph sub-structure.     Dimension Reduction and Coresets for Min-Sum $k$-Clustering

    Authors: Asaf Dallal, Robert Krauthgamer

    Abstract: …, and can be constructed in randomized near-linear-time. We further use these coresets to design streaming algorithms for the problem. ▽ More The min-sum $k$-clustering problem asks to partition an input of $n$ points into $k$ clusters so as to minimize the total pairwise distance within clusters. It is a natural alternative to center-based objectives when the data is not well captured by a small set of representative points. We study for this problem two standard sketching methods, dimension reduction and coresets, which represent the input more compactly while preserving the min-sum cost of every $k$-partition up to $1\pm\varepsilon$ factor. Our first contribution addresses dimension reduction in Euclidean space. The Johnson-Lindenstrauss (JL) Lemma readily implies that a random linear map to target dimension $t=O(\varepsilon^{-2}\log n)$ preserves, with high probability, the min-sum cost of all $k$-partitions. We show that target dimension $t=O(\varepsilon^{-2}(ddim\cdot\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log k+\log\log n))$ suffices for Euclidean inputs of doubling dimension at most $ddim$, and moreover this bound is nearly optimal. We also show that target dimension $t=O(\varepsilon^{-2}(\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log k+\logΔ))$ suffices for Euclidean inputs of aspect ratio at most $Δ$. Our second contribution is to design the first $\varepsilon$-coreset for metric min-sum $k$-clustering. It is actually a movement-based coreset, has size $(1/\varepsilon)^{O(ddim)} k\log n$ for inputs of doubling dimension at most $ddim$, and can be constructed in randomized near-linear-time. We further use these coresets to design streaming algorithms for the problem. △ Less

    Authors: Tianjian Li, Chuanyang Jin, Seungone Kim, Tong Chen, John Hoffman, Yoram Bachrach amd Pedro Rodriguez, Ping Yu, Eryk Helenowski, Swarnadeep Saha, Jack Lanchantin, Weizhe Yuan, Ilia Kulikov, Jason Weston

However, best results are still obtained by research agents interacting with human researchers, and improving AI's ability to perform this co-research will only reinforce this effect---a process termed co-improvement (Weston and Foerster, 2025). Further, aligning with human expectations is likely a safer path for research agents, especially given recent agent reward hacking phenomena (OpenAI, 2026b). In this work we address these issues in four ways: (i) we empirically study the role of humans in directing autoresearch agents, focusing on the domain of benchmark creation, and find humans still play a pivotal role particularly in the ideation phase; (ii) we also develop a benchmark, HackBench, that shows current autoresearch agents are not aligned and are unable to achieve hack localization sufficiently; (iii) we present a method to train agents for improved human interaction by using human simulators as a proxy during reinforcement learning (RL); and finally (iv) we develop a method to improve model text generation, and hence agent communication, by RL with rubrics aligned with human experts. Together, our training and evaluation recipes provide a path toward more capable and aligned (co-)research agents. △ Less
