    Where You Inject Matters: The Trust Region Effect in LLM Agents

    Authors: Chengquan Guo, Xiaodong Liu, Weiwei Yang, Mingqian Feng, Jialin Song, Qiyao Ma, Carolyn Buractaon, Christopher White, Bo Li, Jianfeng Gao

    Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly adopted in long-horizon tasks and interact with diverse external environments. Despite their growing capabilities, these agents remain vulnerable to real-world attacks such as indirect prompt injections, which manipulate agents through malicious instructions embedded in external environments… ▽ More LLM agents are increasingly adopted in long-horizon tasks and interact with diverse external environments. Despite their growing capabilities, these agents remain vulnerable to real-world attacks such as indirect prompt injections, which manipulate agents through malicious instructions embedded in external environments. While existing risk assessment work has primarily focused on the injection content, the effect of where an injection appears has not been systematically characterized. Studying injection placement requires agents to operate over longer-horizon tasks and interact with diverse environmental surfaces. Thus, we build three agentic testbeds spanning tool use, coding, and daily workflow domains with diverse injection surfaces. Holding the injection content fixed, we find that agent susceptibility varies across injection placements. We refer to this phenomenon as the trust region effect: agents implicitly assign different levels of trust to different regions of their environments. For example, an agent powered by GPT-5.4 follows an unsafe injection instruction placed in a repository documentation file (i.e., a README or TODO file) 2.6 times more often on average than the same instruction placed in a code or data file. Building upon this observation, we develop learning-based attackers that generate injection content and select better injection placements. Our approach demonstrates that both where an injection is placed and what content it contains can affect the strength of indirect prompt injection attacks. To the best of our knowledge, this work provides the first systematic characterization for the effectiveness of different injection placements, spanning both coarse-grained environmental surfaces and fine-grained positions. The uneven trust that agents implicitly place across their environments provides important insights for future attack and defense design. △ Less

    Integrable singularities obstruct Cauchy horizons and mass inflation

    Authors: Francisco Tello-Ortiz

    Abstract: …(generalized Vaidya), the gap bounds the blueshift: the advanced time spent in the gapped region is finite, whereas it diverges at a Cauchy horizon. (iii) For counter-streaming null dust, the radiative part $μ$ of the Misner-Sharp mass obeys exactly $\partial_u\partial_vμ=2\,\partial_uμ\,\partial_vμ/(r|f|)$, of which the DTR relation is the thin-shell form;… ▽ More Black-hole interiors with an inner (Cauchy) horizon are unstable through the Poisson-Israel-Ori mass-inflation mechanism, governed by the Dray-'t Hooft-Redmount relation and widely regarded as a dynamical realization of strong cosmic censorship. We show that static, spherically symmetric anisotropic interiors supported by a marginally divergent but integrable density $ρ\sim A/r^2$, with $A>1/(8π)$ and mild screening at finite radius, are incompatible with this mechanism. The compactness $q=m/r$ decreases monotonically from a super-critical central value to $1/2$ at the unique event horizon, producing a strictly positive compactness gap throughout the trapped region and excluding any second zero of the lapse. This gap enters the dynamics quantitatively. (i) Under the null energy condition and a dynamical screening condition, $2m/r$ is non-decreasing along both future null directions of a trapped domain, so the gap propagates under nonlinear spherically symmetric evolution. (ii) For ingoing radiation (generalized Vaidya), the gap bounds the blueshift: the advanced time spent in the gapped region is finite, whereas it diverges at a Cauchy horizon. (iii) For counter-streaming null dust, the radiative part $μ$ of the Misner-Sharp mass obeys exactly $\partial_u\partial_vμ=2\,\partial_uμ\,\partial_vμ/(r|f|)$, of which the DTR relation is the thin-shell form; the in-out coupling $2/(r|f|)$ is uniformly bounded on the interior precisely when the gap is positive. This yields an a priori mass bound and, in the Ori thin-shell model, an e-folding rate bounded by the influx luminosity instead of tending to the inner-horizon surface gravity. Integrable-singularity interiors thus realize a class of sources for which the Poisson-Israel-Ori amplification channel is structurally inoperative in the models in which mass inflation was originally established. △ Less

    cs.LG cs.MA

    Routed Continual Learning

    Authors: Michael Philip, Mahesh Balakrishnan, David Geraghty, David Mao, Vidhya Venkat, Ilya Mironov

    Abstract: …cluster summaries. We also introduce Context Memory Adapter, which mediates between the memory system and the agentic loop. We evaluate the architecture on a heterogeneous stream that interleaves 301 instances from the six CL-Bench tasks with 97 banking instances from $τ^3$-Bench. Across four language models and five matched rollouts, Routed Continual Lear… ▽ More Agents deployed at scale repeatedly encounter related problems, and it is natural to let them learn from each other's experience rather than starting over each time. Unfortunately, there is no single way to retain that experience that works well across settings, and no way to know in advance which future problems a given experience will help with. A global history preserves every detail but becomes expensive and mixes together experience that may not belong together; compressing that history into one fixed representation limits the cost but can throw away valuable details. We introduce Routed Continual Learning (Routed CL), an architecture that decides how to partition and retain experience. Past trajectories are grouped into clusters, each of which maintains state using one memory system from a fixed library. Before each run, an LLM-based router either reuses an existing cluster or creates a new one, basing its decision on the problem statement, memory system descriptions, and cluster summaries. We also introduce Context Memory Adapter, which mediates between the memory system and the agentic loop. We evaluate the architecture on a heterogeneous stream that interleaves 301 instances from the six CL-Bench tasks with 97 banking instances from $τ^3$-Bench. Across four language models and five matched rollouts, Routed Continual Learning raises normalized reward over a single global in-context learning (ICL) history by 15-30% of the maximum attainable normalized reward, achieves higher mean normalized reward than ICL-only clustering, and reduces estimated API cost by 65-72% relative to Global ICL. These results support approaching continual learning as a routing problem that chooses both a learning mechanism and a state cluster rather than committing to one representation globally. △ Less

    ARRAKIHS: The observability of tidally stripped satellites in a Milky Way-like halo

    Authors: Abram Pérez-Herrero, Y. Revaz, S. Roca-Fàbrega, S. T. Nagesh, J. Preston, D. Erkal, D. Herranz Muñoz, O. Agertz, M. Akhlaghi, R. Bieri, A. Camazón-Pinilla, J. Cenarro, A. P. Cooper, H. (12 additional authors not shown)

    Abstract: Deep imaging surveys are revealing increasing numbers of low surface brightness stellar streams around Milky Way-like galaxies. These streams trace the orbital evolution and internal structure of their disrupted dwarf galaxy progenitors. We investigate the evolution and observability of tidal features from dwarf galaxi… ▽ More Deep imaging surveys are revealing increasing numbers of low surface brightness stellar streams around Milky Way-like galaxies. These streams trace the orbital evolution and internal structure of their disrupted dwarf galaxy progenitors. We investigate the evolution and observability of tidal features from dwarf galaxies consistent with the $Λ$CDM framework and Local Group observations. We performed high-resolution $N$-body simulations of dwarf galaxies orbiting in a static Milky Way-like potential comprising a disc, bulge, and dark matter halo. Each dwarf comprised a Plummer stellar distribution embedded in a cuspy or cored generalised Navarro--Frenk--White dark matter halo. Idealised integrated surface brightness maps were constructed every 0.5 Gyr over 10 Gyr, and detectability was quantified through the projected area of stellar light brighter than limiting surface brightnesses, $μ_{\rm lim}=29$--$32$ mag arcsec$^{-2}$, chosen to span depths relevant to ARRAKIHS, LSST, and Euclid. The detectability of tidal features depends most strongly on progenitor stellar mass, orbital configuration, parameterised by the eccentricity proxy $e_0$, and evolutionary stage. Across this range, extended tidal debris is predominantly recovered for initially more radial configurations, mainly those with $e_0 \gtrsim 0.75$. In the simulated grid, detectable area is dominated by $M_\star=10^8$--$10^9 M_\odot$ progenitors, whereas $M_\star=10^6$--$10^7 M_\odot$ systems can undergo stripping without detectable debris. The detectable area evolves non-monotonically with time, reflecting the competition between stream formation, phase mixing, and the fading of diffuse debris below the adopted surface brightness limits. Cuspy and cored progenitors show similar evolution of the detectable area, with differences smaller than the scatter induced by orbital diversity. △ Less

    Hardwired Distributed Processing Units at the Sensor: Truly Parallel Vision Pre-Processing that Frees the Host CPU

    Authors: Raj Mali

    Abstract: …processing units (HDPUs): small, fixed datapaths attached directly to the sensor, each committed to a single always-on task, operating truly in parallel on the same pixel stream and delivering compact results to the host. From one library of 3x3 operators sharing a common two-clock latency, we construct three HDPUs (Canny edges, Harris corners, and a line tr… ▽ More Conventional vision pipelines route every pixel to a host processor, which must then wait for a complete frame before it can act and devote part of each frame period to routine pre-processing. We examine an alternative organisation in which this work is distributed across hardwired distributed processing units (HDPUs): small, fixed datapaths attached directly to the sensor, each committed to a single always-on task, operating truly in parallel on the same pixel stream and delivering compact results to the host. From one library of 3x3 operators sharing a common two-clock latency, we construct three HDPUs (Canny edges, Harris corners, and a line tracker that issues a steering decision) and run them side by side on a single camera stream in a low-cost Artix-7 FPGA, at a cost of 2,568 LUTs and an estimated 0.318 W. Every stage of every unit is bit-exact with its software model. In a full-system simulation with a datasheet camera model, each decision leaves its HDPU within 11.03 camera lines of the pixel that produced it, 22-67x sooner than the earliest moment at which a frame-based program on the host could respond; the HDPU latency varies by no more than 17 ns, whereas the host's own run times reached 12.5x their median. Offloading the three tasks returns 18-30% of a core to the host in every frame period at 640x480, and 63% of the frame period, with all threads in use, at 1080p. The cost of the units grows with frame width only through their line buffers, and in a closed-loop model of a line-following vehicle the HDPU raises the highest speed at which the line is held from 4.75 to 10.00 m/s. △ Less

    AtomTree: A Hierarchical Framework for State-Aware Embodied Instruction Following with LLMs

    Authors: Haotian Zhai, Tianming Sha, Junnan Li

    Abstract: Embodied instruction following in interactive environments requires agents to interpret natural language commands and complete long-horizon tasks. Existing planners either rely on large labeled trajectories, or use few-shot and zero-shot LLM prompting that produces a whole plan in a single serial reasoning process. The… ▽ More Embodied instruction following in interactive environments requires agents to interpret natural language commands and complete long-horizon tasks. Existing planners either rely on large labeled trajectories, or use few-shot and zero-shot LLM prompting that produces a whole plan in a single serial reasoning process. These approaches depend on linguistic common sense, commit to a plan from the initial instruction, and only update after failures, which weakens robustness and state awareness. We present AtomTree, a hierarchical framework for state-aware task planning with LLMs. AtomTree incrementally builds a tree of subgoals and actions, where the LLM decides at each step, from the current symbolic state, whether to further decompose or to output a grounded low-level action. A lightweight memory module summarizes past outcomes for reuse in later decisions. When a low-level action stalls, AtomTree triggers hierarchical backtracking replanning to return to the parent node and regenerate the subtree plan from the latest state, preventing continued drift along an incorrect trajectory. In zero-shot evaluation on the ALFRED benchmark, AtomTree outperforms prior LLM-based planners. Project page: https://zhaihaotian.github.io/atomtree-website/. △ Less

    Streaming Speech Recognition on a Microcontroller: a 65.7M-Parameter Transducer in 2 and 4 Bits on the ESP32-P4

    Authors: Billal Boumedine

    Abstract: We run Kroko, a 65.7M-parameter streaming Zipformer2 transducer for English, on the ESP32-P4, a microcontroller with two 400 MHz RISC-V cores, a 128-bit SIMD extension (PIE) and 32 MB of PSRAM, but no neural accelerator. Without any training, the encoder's 290 matrices are stored with 2 or 4 bits per weight in Hadamard-rotated groups: the width of each m… ▽ More We run Kroko, a 65.7M-parameter streaming Zipformer2 transducer for English, on the ESP32-P4, a microcontroller with two 400 MHz RISC-V cores, a 128-bit SIMD extension (PIE) and 32 MB of PSRAM, but no neural accelerator. Without any training, the encoder's 290 matrices are stored with 2 or 4 bits per weight in Hadamard-rotated groups: the width of each matrix comes from its measured sensitivity, and each matrix is first corrected toward the original model's output, then rounded with GPTQ and a search over the group norms. The released 71 MB of int8 weights become a 26.65 MB file. Activations are int16 between the matrix products and int8 into them. A C engine with hand-written PIE kernels processes each 1.28 s chunk of audio in 0.90 s on both cores, beam search included, and its PC build computes the same bits as the chip, so accuracy is measured on whole test sets. LibriSpeech WER is 4.34% on test-clean and 10.26% on test-other, against 3.94% and 9.17% for the released model; on FLEURS and Common Voice the gap grows to 2.3 and 1.1 points. A GPU simulator shows that the integer arithmetic costs nothing and that the whole gap comes from the weights, and an ablation on all of test-clean shows that GPTQ does most of the work. We also report the port's speed history, from 51.3 s to 0.62 s of encoder time per chunk, each design step measured again in one build, an attempt to reach 16 MB that stopped at 19.63 MB, and what did not work. We did not measure energy. The inference engine, the firmware and the 2/4-bit weights are available at https://github.com/BoumedineBillal/esp32-pie-asr. △ Less

    Kinetic linear stability theory for incompressible parallel shear flows with the full Boltzmann collision operator

    Authors: Vassilis Theofilis

    Abstract: …so that their results can be compared directly. Written in the peculiar velocity about a Chapman-Enskog base state, the collision operators are independent of the cross-stream coordinate and are formed once. Summation-by-parts Legendre collocation, weak diffuse-reflection wall conditions and a moment-consistent velocity derivative yield an energy-stable dis… ▽ More Modal and non-modal linear stability analysis of incompressible shear flows is performed for the first time by linearizing the Boltzmann equation and its full collision operator. In this kinetic linear stability theory (kLST) the hard-sphere operator and its BGK, ES-BGK and Shakhov relaxation models share the same discretization, so that their results can be compared directly. Written in the peculiar velocity about a Chapman-Enskog base state, the collision operators are independent of the cross-stream coordinate and are formed once. Summation-by-parts Legendre collocation, weak diffuse-reflection wall conditions and a moment-consistent velocity derivative yield an energy-stable discrete operator, verified to spectral accuracy by a manufactured solution. Numerical solution of the four-dimensional problem, comparable in size to a TriGlobal problem (Theofilis 2011), is made feasible by the fast spectral method of Gamba, Haack, Hauck and Hu (2017) for the full operator. Modal kLST uses matrix-free shift-invert Arnoldi iteration, preconditioned by an exact relaxation-model inverse. Non-modal kLST is based on an energy norm containing Chu's disturbance energy, a kinetic Reynolds-Orr equation identifying the mean shear as the only energy source. Transient growth and pseudospectra follow from the same preconditioned solves. For plane Couette and Poiseuille flows, the mixing layer and the Blasius boundary layer at Reynolds numbers 10^2-10^5, all four operators recover the Orr-Sommerfeld eigenvalues (Orszag 1971; Mack 1976) in the incompressible limit to a few parts in 10^7 for the models and 10^6 for the full operator. The optimal growth of Butler and Farrell (1992) and the resolvent norms of Trefethen et al. (1993) are reproduced to 0.1%, while the pseudospectra converge where ill-conditioned eigenvalues do not. △ Less

    Geometry Matters in Packed 3D Attention

    Authors: Amil Khan, Anantajit Subrahmanya, B. S. Manjunath

    Abstract: Packing a 3D volume into a token sequence is not merely long-sequence modeling. Once the volume is packed, sequence order no longer encodes the geometry of the sample---so a positional mechanism indexed by sequence position can learn the memory layout rather than the object. This is a general failure mode for attention… ▽ More Packing a 3D volume into a token sequence is not merely long-sequence modeling. Once the volume is packed, sequence order no longer encodes the geometry of the sample---so a positional mechanism indexed by sequence position can learn the memory layout rather than the object. This is a general failure mode for attention over packed geometric data. We introduce PACK3D, a local-global attention operator that separates memory layout from attention geometry. PACK3D keeps local neighborhood attention exact, compresses only distant context into contiguous 3D block summaries, and indexes every query-key interaction with axial RoPE on true token-grid coordinates rather than packed positions. Because the rotation is applied before a block is summarized, the query-to-summary logit remains a weighted sum of query-relative 3D displacements; compressed global context therefore stays spatially interpretable after packing, instead of collapsing to anonymous pooled content. We measure the failure mode directly. On a controlled synthetic task---train on one packing, change only the memory order of the tokens, hold the physical labeling rule fixed---learned positional embeddings and packed-sequence RoPE solve the training layout perfectly yet collapse under Morton, block-contiguous, and random repacking, while axial grid-coordinate RoPE stays exact throughout. The same coordinate system carries to real data: on Allen WTC-11 hiPSC microscopy, PACK3D improves over a parameter-matched 3D U-Net on the 15-structure joint task and over a matched learned-position local-global baseline, with controls isolating the coordinate system, not encoder capacity, as the source of the gain. A fused Triton implementation matches windowed-attention latency at 65k tokens while supplying the long-range context windowing cannot---so the operator is not only correct but trainable at scale. △ Less

    On the Generalization Gap of Streaming Federated Learning with Markovian Data

    Authors: Masoud Kavian, Milad Sefidgaran, Youssef Attia El Hili, Malik Tiomoko, Abdellatif Zaidi

    Abstract: Streaming federated learning (SFL) allows clients to train a shared model from continuously arriving data without retaining large local datasets. Such… ▽ More Streaming federated learning (SFL) allows clients to train a shared model from continuously arriving data without retaining large local datasets. Such streams often exhibit temporal dependence, yet its effect on generalization remains poorly understood. In this paper we study the expected generalization gap of the aggregated model when client data follow Markov processes, using information-theoretic and rate-distortion techniques. Our framework accommodates heterogeneous Markovian dynamics across clients and distinguishes temporal dependence from information acquired during local learning. We establish bounds under cross-entropy loss, specializing them to Linear Next-Token Prediction (LNTP) and Self-Attention Next-Token Prediction (SANTP). These results quantify the effects of client count, communication rounds, sample size, data dimension, mixing time, and context length. In particular, they reveal a communication--generalization tradeoff: although frequent aggregation may help optimization, it can weaken generalization guarantees. They also suggest a potential advantage of distributed over centralized streaming learning under a fixed total data budget. Temporal dependence enters through the square root of the mixing time. By analyzing the history-state process, we obtain refined bounds with improved context-length dependence under suitable mixing conditions. Experiments on GPT-style language-model pretraining with TinyStories and real-world time-series forecasting illustrate how collaboration and communication frequency influence generalization under temporal dependence △ Less

    AskStream: When and What to Ask Proactively under Uncertainty in Streaming Video Understanding

    Authors: Lu Wang, Zhuoran Jin, Longxiang Wang, Junhong Chen, Yupu Hao, Yao Xu, Daojian Zeng, Yubo Chen, Kang Liu, Guang Liu, Jun Zhao

    Abstract: Recent advances in multimodal large language models have increased interest in interaction paradigms for video understanding. The evolution from offline to streaming and proactive interaction has gradually shifted models from passive responders toward more proactive agents that can decide when to answer. However, existing proactive paradigms typically assume… ▽ More Recent advances in multimodal large language models have increased interest in interaction paradigms for video understanding. The evolution from offline to streaming and proactive interaction has gradually shifted models from passive responders toward more proactive agents that can decide when to answer. However, existing proactive paradigms typically assume that the information required to answer a query is complete, leaving models to only wait or answer. In the real world, however, information is often incomplete due to uncertainty from either the video or the user. In proactive paradigms, such uncertainty can cause the model to wait forever or generate highly hallucinatory responses. We therefore argue that proactive interaction should be redefined with a third action: ask, allowing models to actively ask for missing information. To study when and what models should ask before answering, we introduce AskStream, the first multi-turn benchmark spanning video and user uncertainty for streaming video understanding, with four task categories and eight subtasks. It contains 438 videos with 2,714 manually annotated frame-level time windows across three types: wait, ask, and answer. Across both multiple-choice and open-ended settings, even the strongest model attains only 52.18% on our proposed Ask-F1 metric, highlighting substantial limitations in proactive information seeking under uncertainty. △ Less

    CALM: Conflict-Aware Lifecycle Memory Governance for Long-Horizon Language Agents

    Authors: Chang Li, Xianhao Ou, Xinyan Ma, Hao Li, Haoyuan Xu, Jiafeng Liang, Xiaohan Wang, Jiajun Chai, Guojun Yin, Ming Liu

    Abstract: A memory can be outdated for present-day reasoning yet indispensable for reconstructing the past. CALM converts… ▽ More A memory can be outdated for present-day reasoning yet indispensable for reconstructing the past. Relevance-based retrieval overlooks this tension: semantic relevance, evidence-grounded validity, and query-specific eligibility are distinct. We propose CALM, a conflict-aware lifecycle memory governance framework that decouples persistent memory preservation from query-time admission. CALM converts heterogeneous records into evidence-grounded memory cells, updates validity and lifecycle states using support, conflict, and dependency signals, and admits query-appropriate evidence through training-free or learned gating informed by temporal applicability. Across LongMemEval-S and LoCoMo with Qwen and GPT backbones, CALM outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving the best overall accuracy. Compared with simple filtering and reranking, query-conditioned admission further improves evidence selection. Conflict-blocking and robustness analyses show CALM suppresses erroneous retrieved memories while retaining useful evidence. Historical-reconstruction experiments demonstrate that preserving superseded records supports accurate earlier-state reasoning without compromising current-state answers. △ Less

    Recognition of auditory objects from continuous and ultra-sparse spike streams in neuromorphic devices

    Authors: Leonardo E. S. Tavares, Kevin D. Sargent, Jon T. Sakata, Dezhe Z. Jin

    Abstract: …37 ms), sampled from exemplar syllables, transform continuous auditory signals into streams of ultra-sparse spikes that mark the onsets and offsets of features within the signal. Spike patterns elicited by the exemplars are stored as templates. During recognition, template matching is performed using axonal delays between feature-detecting and template-detec… ▽ More Energy consumption is a major concern for modern artificial intelligence (AI) applications. The brain is thought to achieve energy-efficient computation through sparse coding schemes. By emulating the spiking dynamics of the brain, neuromorphic chips hold the potential to realize energy-efficient AI. In this work, we use birdsong to demonstrate that auditory object recognition can be achieved using ultra-sparse coding. Feature-detecting neurons tuned to short temporal features ($\sim$37 ms), sampled from exemplar syllables, transform continuous auditory signals into streams of ultra-sparse spikes that mark the onsets and offsets of features within the signal. Spike patterns elicited by the exemplars are stored as templates. During recognition, template matching is performed using axonal delays between feature-detecting and template-detecting neurons. Recognition is further enhanced by an additional group of specialized binary neurons trained to discriminate between syllable categories with subtle differences. When tested on Bengalese finch songs, our approach achieved error rates below 5% using 10 to 40 exemplars per syllable type. We then implemented this recognition algorithm using spiking neural networks on Intel's neuromorphic chip, Loihi, and found that response latencies remain constant (approximately 17 ms per syllable), regardless of the number of templates used. Our results demonstrate the potential of neuromorphic chips for low-latency, low-energy recognition of complex sounds, including speech. △ Less

    Authors: Nouar AlDahoul, Yasir Zaki

    Abstract: …differently: a lower-status job pushes the base model toward "true" and the post-trained model toward "false"; and 3) this flip is carried by a single residual-stream direction and happens at supervised fine-tuning. However, it remains unclear which post-training changes how much a model's truth judgement depends on the poster's social class, and how such a change happens inside the model. This paper investigates job-based doubt, i.e., judging a claim false more often when the poster has a lower-status job, using fact-checked claims with randomised poster profiles in base and post-trained checkpoints of six model families. Our results reveal that: 1) post-training creates such doubt only with the open Tülu-3 recipe (OLMo-2 32B, Tülu-3 70B), not with Meta's, Alibaba's or Mistral's; the effect is under one percentage point but statistically reliable, exceeding training drift and replicating on independent claim sets; 2) in OLMo-2 32B, the model does not start noticing the poster's job more, but starts interpreting it differently: a lower-status job pushes the base model toward "true" and the post-trained model toward "false"; and 3) this flip is carried by a single residual-stream direction and happens at supervised fine-tuning. The mechanism was confirmed on 400 new claims with pre-specified predictions. None of seven deployed commercial models doubts lower-status posters more. Our work underscores the need to audit speaker bias per training recipe. △ Less

    Accelerating Nonlinear MPC by Behavior Cloning with Separated Timescales: Application to Nuclear Reactor Load-Following

    Authors: Perceval Beja-Battais, Alain Grosset{ê}te, Nicolas Vayatis

    Abstract: …the physical knowledge of multiscale systems in which slow state variables, fast (stiff) state variables, and predictable exogenous inputs evolve at different timescales. On 500 long-horizon roll-outs of a pressurized water reactor (PWR) load-following benchmark -a high-dimensional, stiff industrial system with challen… ▽ More Behavior Cloning (BC) of expert controllers for multiscale dynamical systems is challenging: state variables evolving at widely separated timescales induce highly nonlinear couplings that a shared latent space may fail to adequately represent, degrading closed-loop generalization. We propose Structured Representation Learning for Behavior Cloning (SRLBC), an architecture designed to leverage the physical knowledge of multiscale systems in which slow state variables, fast (stiff) state variables, and predictable exogenous inputs evolve at different timescales. On 500 long-horizon roll-outs of a pressurized water reactor (PWR) load-following benchmark -a high-dimensional, stiff industrial system with challenging safety constraints -and across 20 training seeds, separated embeddings systematically outperform shared embedding baselines on cumulative cost, imitation accuracy and constraint satisfaction. The resulting standalone policies approach NMPC closed-loop performance at a computational cost 15 times lower on average. Since standalone policies still leave a few percent of trajectories infeasible, we embed the learned policies in a safety-augmented approximate NMPC framework, in which the optimizer is only called when the predicted input sequence is not certified as safe. This yields controllers still 6.8 times faster than the NMPC controller on average, with a feasibility (i.e., constraint satisfaction) of at least 99.8% for all architectures, SRLBC retaining the lowest closed-loop cost. Under distribution shift, standalone BC policies degrade sharply: SRLBC degrades the least, and once safety-augmented, it retains a feasibility above 98% with the lowest closed-loop cost. Beyond the quantitative results, Jacobian-based sensitivity analysis shows that the resulting controller relies on a sparse and less redundant set of latent features, each attributable to a single group of physical variables, and aligned with the physical timescale decomposition of the system. △ Less

    GPA-VGGT: Geometry-Preserving Acceleration of VGGT

    Authors: Muyeol Sung, Jangwon Kim, Jongwoo Lim

    Abstract: …pass, making them powerful backbones for multi-view 3D perception. However, VGGT's global attention is quadratic in the number of frames and dominates end-to-end runtime on long sequences, which has motivated a range of training-free efficiency methods such as token merging. These methods, however, come with a drop… ▽ More Feed-forward geometry models such as VGGT recover camera parameters, depth maps, and point maps from one to hundreds of views in a single forward pass, making them powerful backbones for multi-view 3D perception. However, VGGT's global attention is quadratic in the number of frames and dominates end-to-end runtime on long sequences, which has motivated a range of training-free efficiency methods such as token merging. These methods, however, come with a drop in accuracy that makes them hard to use in place of VGGT. We propose GPA-VGGT, a training-free acceleration method that replaces global attention in early blocks with frame attention augmented by a shared cross-frame K/V set. In the remaining blocks, we merge only keys and values while preserving all queries, restore the attention mass of the tokens each merged key represents, and randomly project the matching metric to 128 dimensions to reduce matching cost. Across diverse multi-view geometry tasks, GPA-VGGT provides training-free acceleration at speedups comparable to prior work while preserving the information required for precise geometric estimation, retaining over 99% of VGGT's pose accuracy under AUC@3. △ Less

The same generator supports estimated, native, and on-demand simulator depth, while cached and… ▽ More Continuous visual world simulation requires coupling evolving scene geometry with temporally persistent appearance. We present UniWorld, a unified framework that integrates depth trajectories and reference appearance within a shared video generation model. The central design maintains visual and geometric histories on a common timeline. Noise-aligned overlapping continuation places generated history at the prescribed sampling noise level and commits only newly generated latents. Rolling depth latent state preserves the corresponding geometric controls, incrementally reads newly available geometry, and keeps the conditioning fixed within each sampling window. Together, these mechanisms ensure exact preservation of latent history while bounding the active depth state by the window size. We evaluate continuous generation, paired history interventions, and long-horizon behavior across diverse scene categories. The same generator supports estimated, native, and on-demand simulator depth, while cached and incremental execution paths produce bitwise-identical outputs. Appearance changes and geometric control branches operate through the same interface without modifying committed history. The measured steady-state DiT execution path achieves real-time generation throughput, demonstrating the efficiency of UniWorld for continuous visual world simulation. Project page: https://zephipage.github.io/UniWorld. △ Less

    Authors: Hugo Math, Maximilian Forstenhäusler, Christos Anagnostopoulos

    Abstract: …Critically, current foundation models for DESs are limited to a small, fixed number of event types, restricting their applicability to realistic, high-dimensional event streams. To address this, we propose two complementary modules: (i) DES-JEPA, a joint-embedding predictive architecture that learns a shared discrete vocabulary from synthetic stochastic pro… ▽ More Temporal foundation models such as Chronos and TimesFM generalize across domains through a shared numerical observation space. In contrast, discrete event sequences (DESs), including operational event logs or patient clinical-event trajectories, have domain-specific event vocabularies whose labels lack a consistent correspondence across domains. Beyond this lack of consistency, the number of distinct events in real-world settings is high enough to challenge both classical temporal point process modeling and current in-context foundation inference models such as FIM-PP. Critically, current foundation models for DESs are limited to a small, fixed number of event types, restricting their applicability to realistic, high-dimensional event streams. To address this, we propose two complementary modules: (i) DES-JEPA, a joint-embedding predictive architecture that learns a shared discrete vocabulary from synthetic stochastic processes with unaligned event vocabularies; and (ii) DES-PFN, a discrete event sequence prior-fitted network that reuses the learned vocabulary for in-context next-event prediction. Without task-specific fine-tuning, this framework achieves competitive zero-shot performance on eight unseen real-world datasets and scales up to 700 distinct event types. △ Less

Throughout, it keeps the layout, camera, and every entity's position and timing intact. Our AnyGameRenderer generalizes across a wide range of genres that agents build, such as tower defense, auto-battlers, top-down survivors, turn-based battles, kart racing, and crowd runners. Training uses ~10k paired clips from three sources: (i) multi-stream rendering data from 38 agent-made games; (ii) two-stream rendering data from Unreal Engine, with programmatically controlled crowds, count changes, and entity scales; and (iii) real gameplay recordings, with pairs reconstructed via 3D-box detection. To evaluate the entity correspondence, we propose an entity-level benchmark of 59 held-out cases with 753 entities, scored against ground-truth boxes. Compared with the base model, AnyGameRenderer better preserves entity correspondence: temporal match rises from 50.9% to 62.9%, missed count changes fall from 34.7% to 21.3%, and fewer entities are hallucinated or left unrendered. We in… ▽ More Outcome-based reinforcement learning provides coarse supervision for long-horizon LLM agents: failed trajectories may contain correct steps, while successful trajectories can contain errors. Trajectory-level skills can also shift teacher preferences at unrelated steps, introducing off-target distillation signals. We introduce SCOPE, a framework for Selective Critical-Step On-Policy Self-Distillation. After a supervised warm-up, the policy analyzes its own trajectories, generates step-specific hindsight skills, and distills their guidance only at selected error steps. A bounded signed teacher-student coefficient stabilizes auxiliary updates while preserving positive reinforcement and negative correction. A success-relative pairwise length reward further encourages efficient task completion. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop demonstrate consistent improvements across multiple model backbones. With Qwen2.5-3B, SCOPE achieves 97.7% success on ALFWorld and 85.2% on WebShop, outperforming SEED by 5.9 and 6.3 percentage points, respectively. On unseen ALFWorld tasks, SCOPE reaches 89.6% success, improving over GRPO by 23.2 percentage points with 46.7% fewer interactions. Scaling to Qwen3-32B, SCOPE achieves 99.2% success on seen ALFWorld tasks and 94.0% on unseen tasks. △ Less

    Authors: Xin Huang, Teng Ma, Zhuoran Li, Yibo Zhao, Zhaoshi Li, Qianchao Zhu, Wei Yang, Chenxi Wang, Tao Xie, Youwei Zhuo

    Abstract: …reused at session scope, FlatCake places each block directly onto its target medium in session-contiguous, layer-major order at creation time and never migrates it. FlatCake streams coalesced SSD reads directly into a bounded GPU window via GPUDirect Storage and decouples DRAM and SSD request queues to prevent head-of-line blocking. On a production Claude Co… ▽ More KV cache reuse accelerates LLM serving, but existing systems manage host DRAM and NVMe SSD as a tiered hierarchy where DRAM acts as an intermediate cache. Production LLM workloads break both premises of this hierarchy. First, bimodal reuse defeats DRAM temporal filtering, since workloads are dominated either by one-shot blocks with long reuse distances or by multi-gigabyte agentic sessions that exceed DRAM capacity, putting SSD on the critical read path. Second, DRAM staging and eviction-ordered layout bottleneck SSD restoration. Holding host staging buffers for in-flight prefixes causes contention under load that aborts prefetches, while incremental eviction scatters a session's blocks across the SSD and prevents large sequential reads. We present FlatCake, a flat KV storage architecture that exposes host DRAM and NVMe SSD as peer media in a unified 64-bit address space. Exploiting the fact that KV blocks are write-once, immutable, and reused at session scope, FlatCake places each block directly onto its target medium in session-contiguous, layer-major order at creation time and never migrates it. FlatCake streams coalesced SSD reads directly into a bounded GPU window via GPUDirect Storage and decouples DRAM and SSD request queues to prevent head-of-line blocking. On a production Claude Code trace with DeepSeek-V4-Flash, FlatCake sustains a 94% cache hit rate as load increases (versus 64% for Mooncake), eliminates Mooncake's $6.5\times$--$6.7\times$ SSD read amplification, and reduces peak-load mean TTFT by $1.6\times$ while improving total token throughput by $1.6\times$. Across 8K--128K contexts on Llama-3.1-70B, FlatCake further reduces mean TTFT by up to $2.4\times$ over Mooncake and $1.8\times$ over Mooncake-GDS. △ Less

    Compositional Diffusion Planning with Chunk Selection and Reachability Measure

    Authors: Chanyoung Park, Youngjoong Kim, Jaesik Park

    Abstract: Compositional diffusion planners have demonstrated remarkable success on long-horizon diffusion planning by sampling short trajectories, namely chunks, and composing them into a long trajectory. However, averaging overlapping chunks can induce the mode-averaging problem, resulting in discontinuous trajectories. Several… ▽ More Compositional diffusion planners have demonstrated remarkable success on long-horizon diffusion planning by sampling short trajectories, namely chunks, and composing them into a long trajectory. However, averaging overlapping chunks can induce the mode-averaging problem, resulting in discontinuous trajectories. Several works aim to address this problem, whereas a non-negligible portion of plans remains infeasible. In this work, we propose Selective Compositional Diffuser (SCD), handling this problem by measuring reachability. To this end, we present a self-supervised learning method for the reachability classifier. This is trained on offline data and generated chunks jointly to address potential distributional mismatch, while reducing the bias inherited from the generated data. Based on this, we construct a graph whose edge costs represent whether consecutive chunks are reachable, and traverse the graph to find effective yet efficient routes. Lastly, we generate bridging trajectories and refine the composed trajectory for smooth transitions. We evaluate our method on OGBench stitching tasks. It practically reduces incompatible mode compositions and the mode-averaging problem compared to the studied baselines. Across tasks, SCD improves success rates, reaching state-of-the-art results on HumanoidMaze-Large and Giant tasks. △ Less

    The Role of Quantization in Loss Spikes

    Authors: Zaiqian Chen, Aditya Somasundaram, Sophie Li, Sean McLeish, John Kirchenbauer, Tom Goldstein, Micah Goldblum

    Abstract: …behind their effectiveness. Together, our results identify finite precision error as a hidden driver of time-bomb spikes and show that suppressing this error is key to stable long-horizon pretraining. ▽ More Loss spikes in language model pretraining can waste substantial compute and derail otherwise healthy training runs. A primary driver of these instabilities is the growth and divergence of output logits in the final language modeling (LM) head. In this work, we study a particularly costly form of this instability associated with output logit growth: delayed, terminal divergences that emerge only after prolonged training, which we call time-bomb spikes. We find that these instabilities are preceded by rapidly growing quantization error in the LM head, which eventually culminates in a loss spike. Crucially, reducing numerical precision accelerates time-bomb spikes, while increasing precision improves stability. We then propose a simple yet effective mitigation that replaces AdamW on the LM head with SGD with momentum, suppressing logit growth and improving stability over long training horizons. Finally, we find that existing mitigation techniques from the literature (along with our own) all reduce quantization error, suggesting a shared numerical mechanism behind their effectiveness. Together, our results identify finite precision error as a hidden driver of time-bomb spikes and show that suppressing this error is key to stable long-horizon pretraining. △ Less

    ATI-OPD: Adaptive Teacher Intervention for Stable Long-Sequence On-Policy Distillation

    Authors: Jiannan Guan, Youxin Jiang, Yutai Hou, Qiguang Chen, Yirong Zeng, Haitao Wang, Dengyun Peng, Zheng Yan, Jinhao Liu, Tao Ye, Dandan Tu, Wanxiang Che

    Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own responses using token-level feedback from a teacher. As responses grow longer, the student's prefix can depart from the teacher's own reasoning, weakening its feedback on later tokens. Updates based on this feedback can reinforce the same deviations in later responses and destabilize training. Prior approaches such as length control and loss… ▽ More On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own responses using token-level feedback from a teacher. As responses grow longer, the student's prefix can depart from the teacher's own reasoning, weakening its feedback on later tokens. Updates based on this feedback can reinforce the same deviations in later responses and destabilize training. Prior approaches such as length control and loss reweighting limit learning from problematic responses but leave their prefixes unchanged. Fixed cutoffs can also discard later reasoning. We propose \textbf{Adaptive Teacher Intervention for On-Policy Distillation (ATI-OPD)} to improve subsequent teacher guidance by revising the student's reasoning prefix. ATI-OPD replaces the earliest high-disagreement segment with a short teacher segment, then lets the student resume its own reasoning from the revised text. To handle renewed disagreement, it excludes persistently divergent suffixes from the distillation loss while retaining useful student continuation. Training diagnostics show less teacher-student disagreement, fewer gradient spikes, and fewer extremely long responses. Across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks and two teacher-student pairs, ATI-OPD improves average student-only accuracy over standard OPD. △ Less

It maintains effective contact control over long continuous rollouts and responds to edited schedules such as cadence changes, stop-and-go pauses, limping gaits, and bilateral jumping. Our results show that minimal foot-contact schedules provide an effective and intuitive temporal interface for controllable motion generation. △ Less

    Answer-Only Disclosure: Private-Context LLM Assistance over Web APIs with a Leakage Ledger

    Authors: Liang Yuan, Xiaohu Du, Ruikun Luo, Jinze Wang, Mashud Rana, Kaibin Wang, Jiangwen Zhou, Bo Li, Xiaoyu Xia

    Abstract: …decoding with private drafts did not include the secret code in its answers, yet sent the code to the cloud in rejected drafts. We present ADLER, a decoding protocol for streaming LLM APIs in which every message to the cloud depends only on the public view and the answer the user sees, regardless of how the client combines the two models or how the cloud beh… ▽ More Web assistants increasingly answer questions about a user's private documents by pairing a small on-device model with a cloud LLM behind a Web API. In collaborative decoding, the document stays on the client, and the cloud receives only a public view that the user approves, such as the question alone, but the two models exchange messages while the answer is generated. We show that the message transcript can disclose private content that neither the public view nor the answer contains. In a planted-secret audit, speculative decoding with private drafts did not include the secret code in its answers, yet sent the code to the cloud in rejected drafts. We present ADLER, a decoding protocol for streaming LLM APIs in which every message to the cloud depends only on the public view and the answer the user sees, regardless of how the client combines the two models or how the cloud behaves. A client-side leakage ledger then bounds how much the answer itself reveals relative to a public reference process built from the cloud's replies. In the audit, no ADLER configuration exposed the secret beyond the answer. On GSM8K math problems with numerical values masked in the cloud's input, ADLER with an unlimited leakage budget raises the exact-match accuracy of a 0.5B client from 0.41 to 0.83 and of a 1.5B client from 0.67 to 0.85 under lenient answer parsing, close to the 0.92 achieved by uploading the full problem. It also exceeds a zero-leakage program baseline (0.69) by 0.14 and 0.16, respectively. In this baseline, the cloud writes a program that the client runs offline using the private numbers. The ledger records the disclosure charge associated with this gain; budgets of 16 nats or less leave accuracy near the cloud-only baseline on GSM8K. △ Less

    CADENCE: Distributional Next-Transaction Forecasting for Business Cash-Flow Underwriting

    Authors: Alexander Wu, Jingchao Huang

    Abstract: …ability to pay. For small businesses, recurrence is both indispensable and hard to estimate, because much of their income arrives as ambiguous transfers, most payment streams are short, and schedules follow calendar conventions that frequency-domain methods treat as noise. We present CADENCE, Calendar-Aware Distributional Event Network for Cash-Flow Estimati… ▽ More Recurring cash flows are the backbone of cash-flow underwriting: they separate durable revenue from one-off deposits, expose standing obligations, and ultimately determine ability to pay. For small businesses, recurrence is both indispensable and hard to estimate, because much of their income arrives as ambiguous transfers, most payment streams are short, and schedules follow calendar conventions that frequency-domain methods treat as noise. We present CADENCE, Calendar-Aware Distributional Event Network for Cash-Flow Estimation, a compact causal Transformer that treats each transaction as a token and predicts the amount and a full, nonparametric timing distribution of every cash flow's next payment. Inheriting transaction semantics from an external labeler and a frozen text encoder keeps CADENCE compact yet general across the long tail of small-business counterparties, while predicting a calibrated distribution rather than an opaque embedding makes its outputs interpretable enough to feed existing underwriting models and power credit decisions. Ablations confirm the role of each design choice, with counterparty semantics driving recall on short histories, calendar inputs driving timing accuracy, and no-event supervision driving precision. Against the production FFT baseline, CADENCE improves one-day timing accuracy by 93.3% and next-event F1 by 6.7%, while producing calibrated distributions that serve directly as a confidence gate for downstream decisions. In an end-to-end credit evaluation on production applications, replacing FFT recurrence features with CADENCE features raises KS by 4.8%, raises the approval rate at a fixed loss rate by 18.2%, and lowers the loss rate at a fixed approval rate by 13.1%. △ Less

    Neural Stream Function Method for Electrical Impedance Tomography with Lipschitz Conductivities

    Authors: Xiaosheng Li

    Abstract: …but training physics informed neural networks for EIT and relating their training loss to reconstruction accuracy remain challenging. We develop a novel constrained neural stream function method for planar EIT with Lipschitz conductivities. The method builds the conductivity constraints and prescribed boundary voltages into the neural trial functions and re… ▽ More Electrical impedance tomography (EIT) reconstructs the conductivity inside an object from voltage and current measurements on its boundary. The resulting inverse problem is nonlinear and severely ill-posed. Neural networks offer a flexible computational approach, but training physics informed neural networks for EIT and relating their training loss to reconstruction accuracy remain challenging. We develop a novel constrained neural stream function method for planar EIT with Lipschitz conductivities. The method builds the conductivity constraints and prescribed boundary voltages into the neural trial functions and replaces the second order conductivity equation with a first order system in strong form. The measured current is also used to construct a fixed baseline for the stream function. For this formulation, we derive error estimates that relate the sampled neural objective to errors in the physical boundary currents and the reconstructed conductivity. The error estimates separate the contributions from neural approximation, measurement noise, and sampling. Numerical experiments further demonstrate that the proposed method achieves high reconstruction accuracy with both exact and noisy boundary data. △ Less

    Cooperative EV Charging Assignment Using Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning: A Comparison of QMIX and Independent Q-Learning Under Urban Traffic Uncertainty

    Authors: Nour-Eddine Moumni, Rachid Alaoui, Driss Kiouach

    Abstract: The allocation of electric vehicles to public charging stations is increasingly constrained by the fact that several heterogeneous facilities often serve the same stream of requests while observing only partial local information. An accept/reject decision that is defensible for one station may nevertheless reduce the efficiency of the charging district as a… ▽ More The allocation of electric vehicles to public charging stations is increasingly constrained by the fact that several heterogeneous facilities often serve the same stream of requests while observing only partial local information. An accept/reject decision that is defensible for one station may nevertheless reduce the efficiency of the charging district as a whole. We formulate this operational problem as a Decentralized Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (Dec-POMDP) and examine whether Centralized Training with Decentralized Execution (CTDE), instantiated through QMIX, improves assignment performance relative to a cooperative Independent Q-Learning baseline (IQL). The evaluation uses a six-station urban network simulated with SUMO, five independent random seeds, 200 training episodes, and four traffic flux scenarios ranging from 100 to 250 vehicles/hour. Under this protocol, QMIX reaches a mean reward per evaluated decision of +0.776, versus +0.578 for IQL (delta = +0.198, seed-paired Cohens dz = 2.98, exact two-sided Wilcoxon p = 0.0625, the minimum attainable value at this sample size), and raises the acceptance rate from 72.4% to 94.0% (delta = +21.6 pp, dz = 5.24, Wilcoxon p = 0.0625). A five-variant ablation suggests this gain is primarily attributable to the CTDE architecture when holding cooperative reward terms fixed, though not formally isolated with a single seed. By contrast, cooperative reward shaping and experience sharing show no clear descriptive benefit and may slightly reduce performance. These findings support the view that, for multi-station charging assignment, coordination should be embedded in the learning architecture rather than added only through heuristic cooperation terms. △ Less

    Learning from Relative Action Credit for Escaping Local Minima

    Authors: Giovanni Minelli, Giulio Turrisi, Lorenzo Amatucci, Victor Barasuol, Claudio Semini

    Abstract: …of robots that learn manipulation skills from interaction alone, without pretrained priors or demonstration data. In practice, on-policy methods such as PPO routinely stall on long-horizon, contact-rich tasks: dense reward shaping carries the policy through stages, yet stitching them together and discovering sparsely r… ▽ More Reinforcement learning holds the promise of robots that learn manipulation skills from interaction alone, without pretrained priors or demonstration data. In practice, on-policy methods such as PPO routinely stall on long-horizon, contact-rich tasks: dense reward shaping carries the policy through stages, yet stitching them together and discovering sparsely rewarded events rarely succeeds. We trace this failure to the missing action discrimination in state-value credit assignment: a state-value critic judges progress only by episode outcomes, so when the policy converges to a partial solution all outcomes coincide, and improvement stops because no action can prove better than another. We therefore extend PPO with an action conditioned value critic which, can compare the executed action with alternatives sampled at the same state and aid the learning signal. Combined with Generalized Advantage Estimation and a bound on exploration noise, we show how the extended algorithm escapes the plateau and completes precise peg insertion within a training budget where PPO with identical exploration fails. △ Less

The standard of care to assess progressive disease is contrast-enhanced MRI, which is expensive, claustrophobic, and performed only once every 2-3 months. Wearable accelerometers offer a non-invasive alternative for continuously monitoring patient function between clinic visits. However, accelerometry data streams are typically noisy and highly patient-specific, making conventional time series methods and summary statistics poorly suited to detecting clinically meaningful change. This work aims to classify stable versus progressive disease states by applying rough path signatures for feature extraction from wrist-worn accelerometer data. We convert accelerometer recordings from HGG patients enrolled in the BrainWear study into walking-path segments and use signature kernel maximum mean discrepancy (SigMMD) to quantify change in patients' walking behaviour. We overcome the challenge of inter-patient heterogeneity by adjusting for the natural variation in a patient's walking during stable tumour periods. This patient-specific normalisation substantially improves separation, yielding a candidate biomarker of tumour progression. Our results provide proof-of-concept evidence that signature-based analysis of wearable accelerometry can distinguish stable from progressive disease states, motivating larger longitudinal studies of continuous wearable monitoring as a digital biomarker in neuro-oncology. △ Less

    Seq@K: Evaluating Language Models Under Sequential Feedback

    Authors: Thomson Yen, Gilbert Yang, Naimeng Ye, Minghui Chen, Dong Xia, Tianyi Shen, Yechan Jeon, Yaqin Chen, Daksh Mittal, Fangyu Wu, Hongseok Namkoong

    Abstract: …that measures whether a model can solve a problem within K sequential attempts, provided feedback after each previous failure. Unlike multi-turn agentic benchmarks that examine long-horizon planning, Seq@K isolates and formalizes the core capability of refinement from feedback. We evaluate Seq@K on a suite of 7 benchma… ▽ More Language-model agents differ fundamentally from prior intelligent systems in their ability to learn from natural language in context. This capability enables agents to iteratively refine their answers in response to feedback, a prevalent mode of operation in modern agentic workflows. However, the community primarily ranks models by metrics such as Pass@1 or Pass@K, which cannot capture how they utilize feedback on prior attempts. We introduce Seq@K, a natural evaluation protocol that measures whether a model can solve a problem within K sequential attempts, provided feedback after each previous failure. Unlike multi-turn agentic benchmarks that examine long-horizon planning, Seq@K isolates and formalizes the core capability of refinement from feedback. We evaluate Seq@K on a suite of 7 benchmarks and 16 frontier models, and show that Seq@K reveals a materially different model-selection landscape: under Seq@K, (1) model rankings often differ, (2) the Pareto frontiers admit more efficient configurations and (3) the gains from choosing the optimal model configuration can widen or shrink relative to those of Pass@K. We further show that analyzing model trajectories under Seq@K helps characterize iterative refinement behavior, such as the rate at which models address issues raised in feedback, or how rigidly they hold onto their own answers. Finally, we study how the nature of feedback affects models' iterative refinement, showing that practitioners must carefully design and benchmark the feedback loops of iterative agentic systems. Together, these results establish how Seq@K offers a more faithful metric for intelligent systems that typically operate in such feedback loop, and the necessity to consider Seq@K in evaluating models for these systems. △ Less

    Learning Without Clocks: Partition-Invariant Neural Network

    Authors: Han Wang, Jingyuan Bao, Juntao Wu, Saiao Zhou, Yuman Nie

    Abstract: Event cameras, spiking cochleas and similar sensors emit asynchronous events, yet most learning systems first bin them into frames or packets, so the output depends on how the stream was sliced. Networks whose state is updated exactly at every event and evolves in closed form between events are invariant to this slicing. We ask what more makes the output (i)… ▽ More Event cameras, spiking cochleas and similar sensors emit asynchronous events, yet most learning systems first bin them into frames or packets, so the output depends on how the stream was sliced. Networks whose state is updated exactly at every event and evolves in closed form between events are invariant to this slicing. We ask what more makes the output (i) independent of the processing order of simultaneous events and (ii) continuous in the time stamps, even when two events cross in time, while (iii) staying sensitive to temporal order. For arbitrary nonlinear event models we prove that (i) and (ii) are each equivalent to pairwise commutation of the event updates, and that once updates commute, all order dependence comes from their non-commutation with the inter-event dynamics and vanishes linearly in the time lag. This identifies a class of time-mediated non-commutative (TM) models and yields a resolution-robustness bound for any model. We construct the minimal TM layer, a learnable, provably Lipschitz generalization of the Hassenstein-Reichardt correlator; prove that no single additive layer can uniformly represent ordered pair interactions, whereas a three-dimensional TM state can; show that each TM layer adds two levels of temporal nesting versus one for additive layers; and give a single-layer higher-order construction (TM-k) and a stacking rule preserving all three properties. We instantiate these results as the Clock-Free Event (CFE) layer, linear in the number of events, give a topology-free variant for temporal point processes with closed-form likelihoods, and specify a preregistered clock-robustness protocol with a physical-time threat model for spike-retiming attacks. In preliminary development-set experiments, the TM state beats an otherwise identical additive state by 3.5-9.1 points on DVS128 Gesture and by 24.5 and 30.6 points on SHD and Speech Commands. △ Less

    Universal Short-Time Fourier Transform: Data-Adaptive Window-Length Selection for Time-Frequency Analysis

    Authors: Manan Mittal, Kanad Sarkar, Ryan M. Corey, John R. Buck, Andrew C. Singer

    Abstract: …expectation across the candidate bank yields a single spectrum equivalent to an STFT evaluated with a data-adaptive effective window, while an envelope-normalized synthesis stream guarantees exact overlap-add reconstruction. Evaluations on synthetic and marine bioacoustic signals demonstrate adaptive resolution tracking, enhanced spectral concentration, and… ▽ More The short-time Fourier transform (STFT) characterizes the local frequency content of time-varying signals by evaluating the discrete Fourier transform over a sliding analysis window. The STFT is governed by a fundamental resolution trade-off: a short window localizes rapid transients in time at the cost of frequency resolution, whereas a long window resolves closely spaced harmonics at the expense of temporal resolution. The Universal Short-Time Fourier Transform (U-STFT) resolves this trade-off sequentially by modeling the analysis window duration as a data-adaptive state variable that indexes the number of hops included in the current observation interval. At each frame, a bank of candidate window durations is scored using normalized order-three Rényi entropy to quantify joint time-frequency concentration. A window length trellis aggregates the resulting $\mathcal{O}(3^M)$ candidate window schedules with linear complexity per frame, guaranteeing a cumulative mix-loss regret bound of $\mathcal{O}((k+1)\log M)$ relative to any hindsight window schedule with $k$ resolution changes over $M$ frames. By linearity of the Fourier transform, posterior expectation across the candidate bank yields a single spectrum equivalent to an STFT evaluated with a data-adaptive effective window, while an envelope-normalized synthesis stream guarantees exact overlap-add reconstruction. Evaluations on synthetic and marine bioacoustic signals demonstrate adaptive resolution tracking, enhanced spectral concentration, and exact time-domain invertibility. △ Less

    When Should We Intervene at Test Time?

    Authors: Jiahao Zhang, Xinling Wen, Liangliang Li, Yu Chen

    Abstract: …loses accuracy overall into a useful choice on selected batches. It prevents severe failures caused by changes in arrival order and preserves gains across complete corruption streams. When a fixed rule already works well, PORTAL uses little feedback to retain its performance. Periodic label checks reveal when past evidence needs refreshing and help the contr… ▽ More Test-time adaptation helps a deployed model respond to changing inputs, but the same intervention can rescue one batch and damage the next. Updating normalization statistics, for example, can improve corrupted images yet fail when a batch contains mostly one class. Average accuracy hides these failures and cannot tell a model when to use an intervention. Target labels can guide this decision, but they are scarce and some choices are already clear. We introduce PORTAL, a controller that chooses when to deploy an intervention, which inputs to label, and when to stop querying. Each available prediction rule is a candidate, and its predictions are fixed before current feedback arrives. Every acquired label therefore evaluates all candidates on the same input. PORTAL combines these shared comparisons and directs queries toward candidates that can still overtake the current leader. It stops when the remaining labels cannot change the choice or when the possible accuracy gain no longer justifies another query. Our analysis links the required feedback to accuracy gaps and shared mistakes, explaining why some choices need much less evidence. Experiments show that this control turns an intervention that loses accuracy overall into a useful choice on selected batches. It prevents severe failures caused by changes in arrival order and preserves gains across complete corruption streams. When a fixed rule already works well, PORTAL uses little feedback to retain its performance. Periodic label checks reveal when past evidence needs refreshing and help the controller recover as conditions change. The acquired labels also train stronger candidates for future batches, and selection brings nearly all of their accuracy gains into deployment. The same feedback therefore improves both the choice made now and the predictions available to later batches. △ Less

    Authors: Ian Chuang, Hiya Shah, Vamsi Chilamkur, Iman Soltani, Claire Tomlin

    Abstract: …a task that demands diverse and difficult grasps: moving an object to almost any position and orientation within reach of the fingers, at any wrist orientation, through long sequences of consecutive goals. We present a recipe for training such policies for a LEAP hand in simulation without human data, built on a range… ▽ More Dexterous manipulation with multi-fingered hands is the next frontier of robotics, yet learned hand skills remain limited to simple grasps or basic repeated motions, such as rotating an object. As robots attempt more demanding in-hand maneuvers, safety, such as not dropping the object, becomes critical, yet it is rarely measured. We study in-hand object pose reaching, a task that demands diverse and difficult grasps: moving an object to almost any position and orientation within reach of the fingers, at any wrist orientation, through long sequences of consecutive goals. We present a recipe for training such policies for a LEAP hand in simulation without human data, built on a range of insights, from diverse resets and goals via generated grasp datasets to an adaptive friction curriculum. With careful system identification, the policies transfer zero-shot to the real hand, using either motion capture or a single wrist-mounted depth camera, and prove remarkably capable and robust. On top of the task policy, we add a learned residual safety filter with adjustable conservativeness, letting us tune the balance between performance and safety and greatly reduce object drops. In 13.3 hours of real-robot evaluation over 4,800 unseen goals across 4 objects and diverse wrist orientations, the state-based policy reaches 95.2% of goals within 2 cm and 0.4 rad, and the filter cuts its drops by 75% while still reaching 91.5% of goals. The policies can also spin a lightbulb held in an arbitrary pose, reach out-of-distribution goals, withstand fast arm motion and perturbations, and, needing only a wrist camera, serve as portable skills such as screwing in a lightbulb. We release the full recipe as a platform for studying performance, reliability, and safety in high-dimensional dexterous systems. Videos and more are available at https://ian-chuang.github.io/dexsafe/. △ Less

    Multi-State Linear Attention with Generalized Second-Order Routing

    Authors: Hee Seung Hwang, Sanghyuk Chun, Xindi Wu, Olga Russakovsky, Zhiwei Deng

    Abstract: Self-attention plays a key role in long-sequence modeling, but its quadratic time complexity fundamentally limits the scaling of Large Language Models. Recently, Multi-State Linear Attention (MSLA) mechanisms have been proposed to make sequence modeling more efficient by splitting the context into segments and compress… ▽ More Self-attention plays a key role in long-sequence modeling, but its quadratic time complexity fundamentally limits the scaling of Large Language Models. Recently, Multi-State Linear Attention (MSLA) mechanisms have been proposed to make sequence modeling more efficient by splitting the context into segments and compressing each in its own memory state. MSLA aggregates the information extracted from each memory state using only the mean alignment between a query and each segment's keys, ignoring how the alignment is distributed within the segment. In order to model the relevance of a segment to a query more comprehensively and incorporate the variance of the alignment in the aggregation, we propose MERLIN (MomEnt Routing for multi-state LINear attention), a method that tracks the first and second moments of each segment's keys. Using the moments of keys, a query can recover both the mean and the variance of its alignment with each segment to perform a more content-aware aggregation of per-state readouts. Experiments on multi-state variants of Gated DeltaNet-1.3B and Mamba-2-780M show that MERLIN consistently outperforms existing aggregation methods on various long-context tasks. In particular, MERLIN outperforms baselines on RULER needle-in-a-haystack and long-context QA (+8.3% for Gated DeltaNet-1.3B and +7.0% for Mamba-2-780M) and diverse subtasks in LongBench (+13.5% and +12.0%). It even outperforms baselines that consume 4x more memory. MERLIN provides a simple and general routing mechanism for improving long-context capabilities of MSLA models. △ Less

    Authors: Shuolin Xu, Guanyuan Li, Boyu Li, Kelu Yao, Xiaogang Xu, Hongchuan Yu, Shuicheng Yan, Xiaobin Hu

Encoding references independently of the current scene provides little guidance for this integration, while keeping their features in persistent memory can interfere with established context. We propose ReferStream, a framework that requires no additional training and combines reference construction using the current scene with transient cache injection. Joint Latent places reference latents in target regions of the latest scene latent, allowing reference features to be extracted in context. Cache Restoration uses these features to condition the injection chunk, then restores the overwritten global cache entries while retaining the newly generated history. This procedure incorporates new visual conditions without retraining the generator or revising emitted frames. We further introduce ReferStream Bench, a new benchmark with scheduled reference arrivals, evolving instructions, and explicit annotations of subject presence for InShot and MultiShot generation. Evaluation follows the event schedule and measures both the fidelity of new subjects and the preservation of established subjects, alongside video quality, text adherence, and count accuracy. Experiments on ReferStream Bench show higher scores for text adherence, subject similarity, and count accuracy than the compared baselines, including offline methods given all references in advance. Project page: https://xushuolin.github.io/ReferStream/. △ Less

    Strategyproof Deep Reinforcement Learning for Online Resource Allocation

    Authors: Fariba Dehghan, Sebastian Stein, Vahid Yazdanpanah, Stephanie Gauthier

    Abstract: …ensure strategyproofness and individual rationality, while the neural rule provides flexibility and imitation improves training efficiency. Empirically, on a four-resource stream with two partner pairs that a linear difference cannot represent, the neural rule achieves 97.8% of an offline welfare upper bound, compared with 93.8% for a linear learner and 89.0… ▽ More Online resource allocation faces two coupled challenges: (1) allocating scarce resources efficiently under uncertain future demand, and (2) ensuring that agents report task valuations and requirements truthfully (strategyproofness). Reinforcement Learning (RL) adapts allocation decisions through experience but does not necessarily ensure strategyproofness. Existing strategyproof RL uses a linear allocation rule, which limits the resource interactions it can represent. Neural mechanism design methods offer greater expressiveness, but provide only approximate incentive guarantees. Against this background, we introduce the first strategyproof deep RL mechanism for online resource allocation. It integrates three novel components: (i) a monotonic neural network that learns nonlinear interactions by construction (higher reported value cannot reduce acceptance, while a longer reported duration or larger resource requirement cannot increase it); (ii) a generalised critical payment: the lowest accepted value, with all other inputs fixed; and (iii) training by either RL or imitation of an existing rule. Together, monotonicity and the critical payment ensure strategyproofness and individual rationality, while the neural rule provides flexibility and imitation improves training efficiency. Empirically, on a four-resource stream with two partner pairs that a linear difference cannot represent, the neural rule achieves 97.8% of an offline welfare upper bound, compared with 93.8% for a linear learner and 89.0% for first-come, first-served, closing 65% and 80% of the remaining optimality gap, respectively. Moreover, imitation reaches 97.5% in only 261 seconds, about 89 times faster than RL. △ Less

This is difficult in long-horizon workflows, where agents must use information across applications and useful operations are interleaved with failed attempts. We introduce Task Advancement Memory (TAM), which defines task advancement as verified task progress, and supports ongoing task execution and learns reusable operations. During execution, Task Advancement State represents how verified task progress changes the remaining goals, allowing the agent to build on earlier achievements across applications. After each task, advancement supervision identifies and abstracts the interactions that produced a verified local result. The learned operations preserve the relation between required inputs, expected outcomes, and verification conditions. This retains locally successful operations from both successful and failed tasks. On MobileWorld GUI-only tasks, Advancement State alone raises success from 12.0% to 28.2%. Full TAM achieves a 17.6% relative gain in task success over the strongest evaluated baseline and reduces lifecycle tokens per solved task by 38.7%.     Beyond the Readout: A Latent Numerical Frame Shared Across Modality and Operation

    Authors: Oriol Juan Sabater, Alex Ferrando de las Morenas, Pau Rodríguez, Xavier Suau

    Abstract: …output digits. We therefore use causal interventions.
