The subspaces answer three questions. First, do the conditions write numbers along the same directions? They do not. Re… ▽ More When a multimodal model computes 17+35 from text or 91-39 from a rendered image, it produces the same answer, 52. Does it use one internal concept of number for both, or separate machinery for each path? Similarity metrics such as RSA or CKA cannot settle this question. They give the same score to identical and to rotated directions, and two representations can look alike only because both prepare the same output digits. We therefore use causal interventions. For four conditions (text or image, addition or subtraction), we learn a small subspace of Gemma4-12B-it's residual stream in which swapping coordinates swaps the model's answer. The subspaces answer three questions. First, do the conditions write numbers along the same directions? They do not. Reusing a subspace from text in an image on the same operation recovers only 53.2% of its causal effect. A single rotation and rescaling maps one subspace onto the other and raises recovery to 91.4%, so the conditions share one geometry expressed in different coordinates. Second, does this shared geometry only reflect the digit the model is about to print? To test this, we split each subspace into 9 directions that the output layer reads to choose the next digit and 13 directions that it cannot see. The 13 hidden directions still carry the number, yet changing them barely alters the digit being written. Third, what are these hidden directions for? A model must emit a multi-digit answer one digit at a time, so it can compute the full result early and keep it in reserve. When two numbers share their first digit, swapping the number in the hidden directions leaves the digit being written intact and redirects the next digit in 77.6% of cases. The model thus forms an abstract numerical concept upstream and unfolds it across time: multimodal representations share meaning through relational geometry across distinct coordinate frames. △ Less

However, these have focused on single hydrodynamical codes with a number of limitations, making it unclear how generic this result is. We compare satellite disruption in four models: two cosmological hydrodynamical simulations (Auriga and FIRE), cosmological $N$-body simulations with stellar particle tagging (Aquarius), and a semi-analytic model with varying host disk properties (SatGen). Satellites in Auriga experience substantially more mass loss than other simulations, which we attribute to their massive central galaxies and large satellite sizes prior to infall. In the ultra-faint regime captured by Aquarius and SatGen, disruption rates are comparable to or exceeding those for classical satellites. The ubiquity of mass loss across models presents both an exciting opportunity (a plethora of stellar streams awaiting discovery) and a nuisance (for theorists using satellite counts to constrain dark matter). △ Less

PBNs therefore close a longstanding gap in computational neuroscience by combining two properties that have been largely separated: the brain-inspired learning mechanisms of predictive coding and the representational power of modern deep networks.     cs.SE cs.AI

    Spine: Compiling Subtask Trees into Structured Context for Long-Horizon Coding Agents

    Authors: Jiahong Xiang, Kunqiu Chen, Lingming Zhang, Yuqun Zhang

    Abstract: Long-horizon coding tasks contain nested subtasks. Typically, agents record interactions sequentially into a growing history, causing context inflation and performance degradation. Compressing or trimming the history without explicit subtask boundaries can discard information needed by unfinished subtasks. Conversely,… ▽ More Long-horizon coding tasks contain nested subtasks. Typically, agents record interactions sequentially into a growing history, causing context inflation and performance degradation. Compressing or trimming the history without explicit subtask boundaries can discard information needed by unfinished subtasks. Conversely, recursive subagent delegation isolates completed subtasks through child agents by assigning every subtask boundary to delegation, which conflates subtask granularity with executing agent choice, adding execution and coordination overhead. We introduce Spine, an interface and runtime that align model context with subtask nesting and completion. An agent declares explicit subtask boundaries through work scopes, allowing the same agent to execute nested subtasks inline while reserving delegation for independent parallel tasks. The runtime organizes execution in SpineTree, a persistent tree recording subtask relations, local interactions, completion states, and returned results. Before each model call, it compiles the tree into model context just in time, retaining unfinished subtasks' interactions and replacing completed subtasks' details with their results, while preserving prefix-cache reuse. We integrate Spine into Codex to build SpineCodex. With GPT-5.6 Sol, compared with native Codex, SpineCodex improves SWE-Milestone Score by 11.8% while reducing total cost by 32.4%, improves the average ProgramBench test-pass rate by 17.3%, and improves Mean@5 on eight of the ten evaluated FrontierSWE tasks. On SWE-Milestone, it also achieves a comparable Score to Recursive Subagent at 73.7% lower total cost. We also integrate Spine into Pi through a reusable software package: with Gemini 3.8 Flash and Qwen 3.8 27B, it improves SWE-Milestone Score by 9.5-17.5% and the average ProgramBench test-pass rate by 4.3-8.7% compared with native Pi. △ Less

    cs.AI

    Does Your Generalist Skill Optimizer Really Incentivize Agentic Tasks? Rethinking Skill Optimization via Bipartite Attribution

    Authors: Jiawei Zhou, Bo Peng, Yuxuan Huang, Ming Zhang, Youbang Sun, Xiang Wang, Chaochao Lu

    Abstract: Skill optimizers adapt an agent to long-horizon tasks, such as repairing a terminal environment, by rewriting its skill, a natural-language procedure in its context, and keeping the edits that raise success on a small dev set. On such tasks success is noisy, so a gain on a small dev set does not show that an edit helps… ▽ More Skill optimizers adapt an agent to long-horizon tasks, such as repairing a terminal environment, by rewriting its skill, a natural-language procedure in its context, and keeping the edits that raise success on a small dev set. On such tasks success is noisy, so a gain on a small dev set does not show that an edit helps, and a success score does not show what the edit costs. We introduce BiPA, which replays an optimizer's acceptance rule on candidates identical to its seed to measure what selection alone reports, and records each episode as a bipartite graph of turns and the text they read to trace extra input tokens to the behavior behind them. Across three optimizers, three agentic benchmarks and two models, the learned skills succeed about as often as their hand-written seed, 0.1 points higher on average. Their dev gains vanish on test: keeping the best of several candidates on at most 30 tasks reports gains this large even when none changed. They read about 16% more input tokens: the optimizers almost always add rules, half to re-check work and some to act one call at a time, and the agent follows them as extra turns, each re-sending the whole conversation. Four principles follow: accept an edit only if its gain exceeds what selection alone reports; charge it for the turns it adds, not for its length; let the editor delete and merge rules; show it what earlier rules cost. Code is available at https://github.com/valleysprings/BiPA.git. △ Less

    PipeLink: Contract-Guided Linking of GPU Kernel Components

    Authors: Jize Guo, Xupeng Miao

    Abstract: …machine-learning kernels are compositions of components, such as loaders, matrix-multiply mainloops, epilogues and communication stages, that run concurrently on one GPU streaming multiprocessor and share its on-chip memory, barriers, warps and registers. Because these components hard-code where they sit in this shared space, they cannot be compiled, shipped… ▽ More The fastest machine-learning kernels are compositions of components, such as loaders, matrix-multiply mainloops, epilogues and communication stages, that run concurrently on one GPU streaming multiprocessor and share its on-chip memory, barriers, warps and registers. Because these components hard-code where they sit in this shared space, they cannot be compiled, shipped or replaced on their own: combining components from different sources means forking them by hand or keeping them in separate kernels. We present PipeLink, a contract-guided linker for GPU kernel components. Each component is compiled separately into an LTO object that carries a contract stating what it requires and what it promises; PipeLink checks the contracts against each other, relocates every component by solving one joint placement of the SM's resources, and generates the transfers between them, and the vendor LTO backend folds the result into constants. Across 51 configurations on Hopper and Blackwell GPUs, linked kernels run within 0.2% of the same sources compiled as one unit. Linking makes new compositions practical: a CuTe attention operator inside a ThunderKittens decode megakernel speeds up decoding by 2.16$\times$, a four-owner MoE training megakernel matches the original monolith, one unchanged attention core yields sparse and context-parallel variants that outperform FlexAttention, vLLM and Transformer Engine, and a GEMM + SwiGLU composition whose consumer serves three GPU architectures runs 1.2$\times$ faster on Blackwell than cuBLAS with a compiled activation. △ Less

    Action-Value Calibration via Continuation Sharing for Long-Horizon Agentic Reinforcement Learning

    Authors: Yingjian Chen, Lang Zhou, Zixin Zhao, Pingfan Li, Weishi Zheng, Zhilin Zhao

    Abstract: Reinforcement learning for long-horizon agents relies on feedback from sequences of interactions, making credit assignment to individual actions difficult. Existing methods such as GiGPO compare actions at shared states to compute relative advantages, but estimate each action's value from a single sampled continuat… ▽ More Reinforcement learning for long-horizon agents relies on feedback from sequences of interactions, making credit assignment to individual actions difficult. Existing methods such as GiGPO compare actions at shared states to compute relative advantages, but estimate each action's value from a single sampled continuation. The resulting local credit remains sensitive to decisions made later in the trajectory. To address this problem, we propose ConCal (Continuation Calibration), a recursive continuation-value estimator that incorporates continuation evidence already present in a rollout batch. ConCal matches visits from the same task by their incoming state-action-state transitions, combines shared continuation evidence with each visit's own transition estimate, and propagates calibrated values backward to refine earlier action-value estimates. This reduces dependence on individual sampled continuations and mitigates local credit distortions without additional rollouts. Held-out return diagnostics show lower error in within-group value differences and higher agreement with reference action orderings than estimates based on individual continuations. With Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, ConCal improves success rates over GiGPO by 3.39 and 5.86 percentage points on ALFWorld and WebShop, respectively. Our code is available at https://github.com/Yingjian-Chen/ConCal. △ Less

    Estimation and Inference for One-pass Streaming Function-on-Function Regression

    Authors: Shuyang Jiang, Xin Liu, Peijun Sang

    Abstract: In many applications, functional pairs are collected sequentially from a data stream. However, this procedure may be infeasible when observations cannot be stored. In this paper, we propose a one-pass… ▽ More In many applications, functional pairs are collected sequentially from a data stream. Existing function-on-function regression methods are primarily designed for batch data, and bootstrap inference has become one of the most popular approaches. However, this procedure may be infeasible when observations cannot be stored. In this paper, we propose a one-pass functional SGD estimator that updates upon each observation's arrival without data storage, and a weighted rolling validation rule is provided for step size selection for implementation. Further, an online multiplier bootstrap is developed for inference that updates perturbed stochastic gradient paths in parallel, bypassing estimating an infinite-dimensional covariance operator. Asymptotic behaviors are established for the proposed method, and conditional bootstrap validity is justified for the bias-centered pointwise estimation error. Simulation studies and two real data applications confirm the empirical effectiveness of the proposed procedures. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first theoretically justified one-pass framework for bootstrap inference on a bivariate coefficient surface with streaming functional data. △ Less

    Authors: Francesco Benocci, Leonard Dahlmann, Francesco Gargiulo, Tobias Heuer, Silvio Martinico, Franco Maria Nardini, Cosimo Rulli, Rossano Venturini

    Abstract: …for live HNSW routing. By applying offline graph permutations to artificially induce structural locality, we enable the use of SIMD-accelerated integer compressors, such as Stream VByte, for rapid on-the-fly decompression. Evaluated across diverse real-world workloads, we significantly outperform the current state of the art in both compression ratio and que… ▽ More Graph-based Approximate Nearest Neighbor (ANN) indexes, such as HNSW, are the backbone of modern vector search. However, their massive memory footprint creates a severe infrastructure bottleneck. While vector quantization is widely adopted to reduce the memory required to store data vectors, the graph's edge structure increasingly dominates total memory usage. Compressing these adjacency lists for online search presents a critical system challenge: because decompression occurs during query processing, i.e., while navigating the graph, memory savings cannot come at the expense of query latency. In this paper, we present a compression technique explicitly tailored for live HNSW routing. By applying offline graph permutations to artificially induce structural locality, we enable the use of SIMD-accelerated integer compressors, such as Stream VByte, for rapid on-the-fly decompression. Evaluated across diverse real-world workloads, we significantly outperform the current state of the art in both compression ratio and query latency. Our experimental results show that we achieve compression ratios up to 52.7% with negligible penalties in latency compared to the uncompressed baseline. Furthermore, our technique has been effectively deployed in production at eBay, achieving up to 44.3% reduction in edge storage with no degradation in query latency. △ Less

    Authors: Shang-Ran Huang, Fu-Shun Hsu, Ji-Chang Huang, Chuan Chen, Shou-Zen Fan, Chang-Fu Su, Eng-Che Huang, Yuan-Ren Cheng

It refines the detected inhalations by removing incomplete events, low-energy false positives, and double counts, concatenates them i… ▽ More Tracheal auscultation is recommended by clinical practice guidelines, and required by some U.S. state regulations, for monitoring ventilation during procedural sedation and monitored anesthesia care. However, continuous auscultation with a conventional stethoscope is impractical, susceptible to ambient noise, and difficult to interpret. We developed the Artificial Intelligence Respiratory MOnitoring Device (Airmod), an FDA-cleared software as a medical device (510(k) K242798) that continuously monitors tracheal sounds and estimates breath-to-breath respiratory rate in real time. In this paper, we describe its software architecture and key processing modules. Ambient noise is suppressed by active noise control, using a normalized least-mean-square adaptive filter with a dual-microphone electronic stethoscope. A multiclass convolutional bidirectional gated recurrent unit (CNN-BiGRU) model detects inhalations and exhalations in 15-s inference windows. It was trained on HF_Tracheal_V2, a new database of 373 continuous tracheal sound recordings (8,521 min; 107,644 labeled inhalations) from 369 subjects under sedation. To handle continuous acoustic streaming, Airmod runs inference on overlapping 15-s windows. It refines the detected inhalations by removing incomplete events, low-energy false positives, and double counts, concatenates them into a history list, and estimates respiratory rate from the mean period of the last three breaths. On the test set, at the default 2-s prediction interval with refinement enabled, inhalation detection achieved a sensitivity of 0.828, a positive predictive value of 0.832, and an F1-score of 0.830. Airmod also broadcasts the noise-cancelled tracheal sound and displays a real-time spectrogram to help clinicians recognize respiratory abnormalities. △ Less

    StreamSE: Neural Audio Codec-based Streaming Speech Enhancement for On-device Inference

    Authors: Hyunjin Choi, Zinu Kim, Junwon Moon, Jeongyun Chae, Seungbeom Kim, Kyuhong Shim

    Abstract: Recent generative speech enhancement (SE) models achieve high perceptual quality, but their non-causal processing and expensive computation make them difficult to use for streaming, on-device inference. We propose StreamSE, a 20M-parameter… ▽ More Recent generative speech enhancement (SE) models achieve high perceptual quality, but their non-causal processing and expensive computation make them difficult to use for streaming, on-device inference. We propose StreamSE, a 20M-parameter streaming SE model that operates in the latent space of a frozen pretrained neural audio codec. At each frame, StreamSE directly regresses a clean continuous latent from the noisy continuous latent while conditioning on its own predictions from previous frames. Enhancement therefore requires a single forward pass per frame and no iterative sampling or look-ahead. We further truncate the codec's residual vector quantizer (RVQ) to its coarse levels, halving the number of tokens per frame while improving robustness to reverberation. StreamSE is the only streaming generative model in our comparison, yet it matches or exceeds the perceptual quality of the offline generative baselines. Furthermore, the complete StreamSE pipeline runs in real time on a single smartphone CPU core. Our results show that continuous latent regression provides a practical route to generative SE under fully causal, on-device streaming constraints. △ Less

    Local Metrics for Exact Streaming on Curvilinear Lattices: A Curvilinear D3Q27 Cumulant Lattice-Boltzmann Method

    Authors: Maher Sawaf, Jonas Latt, Nikolaus A. Adams, Mostafa S. Shadloo

    Abstract: A three-dimensional curvilinear lattice-Boltzmann method is introduced for simulations on smoothly deformed grids while retaining exact one-to-one streaming on a uniform computational lattice. The coordinate transformation is represented locally through a metric-Gaussian equilibrium and a Christoffel-connection source in moment space. A dynamically reconstru… ▽ More A three-dimensional curvilinear lattice-Boltzmann method is introduced for simulations on smoothly deformed grids while retaining exact one-to-one streaming on a uniform computational lattice. The coordinate transformation is represented locally through a metric-Gaussian equilibrium and a Christoffel-connection source in moment space. A dynamically reconstructed homogeneous-state perturbation streaming procedure preserves homogeneous physical states on warped meshes without interpolation in the advection step. The method is implemented on a D3Q27 lattice with a cumulant collision operator, while the geometric construction remains compatible with other local collision models. The formulation is assessed using two sets of tests. First, static non-orthogonal warped-cube cases validate the bulk curvilinear solver through a convected helical shear wave, the Taylor--Green vortex, and Arnold--Beltrami--Childress flow. These tests verify viscous decay, clean freestream preservation, vortex-breakdown dynamics, and the nonlinear inertial--pressure balance induced by geometric acceleration. Second, wall-bounded and moving-boundary demonstrations assess the boundary-compatible extension through warped plane Poiseuille flow, arbitrary Lagrangian--Eulerian (ALE) peristaltic pumping, and a two-way fluid--structure interaction (FSI) case with added-mass frequency shift. The results show that the proposed curvilinear LBM avoids interpolation-based transport, reduces geometry-locked numerical modes, preserves global mass and momentum to round-off accuracy in the tested warped periodic cases, and extends to wall-bounded ALE/FSI configurations with conservative treatment of mass and physically consistent momentum exchange. △ Less

    Live-OPSD: On-Policy Self-Distillation for Proactive Streaming Large Video Models

    Authors: Kaixuan Du, Xin Wan, Dai Guan, Ming Chen, Xiaoxi Jiang, Guanjun Jiang

    Abstract: Streaming multimodal assistants must make response decisions while events are still unfolding. They need to recognize when the available evidence warrants a response, produce an appropriate continuation, and remain silent otherwise. Supervised fine-tuning learns from recorded assistant turns, but does not directly train on the response prefixes produced by the evolving policy itself.     Tail Cues, Principal Corrections: Plug-and-Play Rectification for Open-Set Test-Time Adaptation

    Authors: Yingkai Yang, Chaoqi Chen, Hui Huang

We present TALOR, a lightweight, backward-free rectification modu… ▽ More Open-Set Test-Time Adaptation (OSTTA) operates on non-stationary target streams where covariate and semantic shifts coexist. Existing TTA methods often obtain adaptation signals by updating model parameters or maintaining mutable memories, which can be costly and vulnerable to unknown samples. We present TALOR, a lightweight, backward-free rectification module that uses the source linear head as a geometric anchor. The head-induced basis decomposes each normalized feature into high-gain principal coordinates and low-gain tail coordinates. TALOR estimates a soft-routed affine correction in the principal subspace, combining a regime-level principal bias with a tail-conditioned slope that captures sample-level tail-to-principal coupling. Using weighted ridge regression, it subtracts this drift estimate from the principal coordinates, reconstructs the feature and feeds it into the source head. On the ImageNet-C benchmark with six csOOD datasets, TALOR outperforms the next-best UniEnt by 2.2% H-score while running 2.5x faster and using only 12% of its GPU memory; as a post-correction plug-in, it further boosts COME to 68.4% H-score with only 2% additional memory overhead. △ Less

However, HQC's encapsulation does not split as easily into individual packets, as cyclic polynomial multiplication, unlike matrix-vector multipli… ▽ More Stateless post-quantum authenticated key exchanges such as post-quantum WireGuard hedge against unexpected cryptanalytic advances by combining a lattice KEM with code-based Classic McEliece. However, recent attacks have led Germany's BSI to advise against McEliece, which leaves these protocols with lattices alone. HQC, the code-based KEM that NIST selected, could restore diversity, but its keys do not fit in a single packet, so a server would have to buffer multi-packet handshakes. This would leave an attack surface for memory exhaustion, which is what WireGuard's stateless handshake was designed to avoid in the first place. McTiny demonstrated that stateless key encapsulation for Classic McEliece is possible by streaming key blocks one-at-a-time. However, HQC's encapsulation does not split as easily into individual packets, as cyclic polynomial multiplication, unlike matrix-vector multiplication, is inherently non-local. We present HqcLethe, a stateless key exchange that streams an HQC encapsulation in packet-sized pieces. It uses the Chinese remainder theorem to split the polynomial multiplication into independent remainders, one per packet, and masks them so that they reveal no more than an ordinary HQC ciphertext, which we prove in Rocq with SSProve; we also verify session secrecy, server authentication and forward secrecy in ProVerif. Client puzzles bound CPU exhaustion, and HqcLethe removes memory exhaustion: on a Raspberry Pi 3B+, each worker runs in a fixed 1.2-1.6 MiB. At category 5 it sends 27x fewer bytes and uses 9.0x less server CPU than McTiny, the earlier stateless code-based design. △ Less

    cs.LG cs.AI cs.CL

    Select-TTT: Query-Conditioned Selective Fast Weight Updates for In-Place Test-Time Training

    Authors: Yue Wu, Yang Zhang, Weinan Gan, Chongming Gao, Xinsen Guo, Xiaoyuan Li, Fuli Feng

    Abstract: …findings highlight the value of query-conditioned fast weight writes for practical test-time training on long-context question answering, which is also potentially promising for long-horizon agent tasks involving multiple user queries over evolving contexts. ▽ More Test-time training (TTT) enables a deployed language model to internalize incoming context on-the-fly into a small set of fast-weight parameters, rather than merely treating it as transient context for attention. In-Place TTT recently made this idea practical for large language models by repurposing the down-projection of standard MLP blocks as fast weights. However, we find that its benefit largely disappears on realistic web long-context question answering. We attribute this to In-Place TTT's uniform write schedule: every chunk is written with equal strength, so irrelevant content dilutes query-relevant information in fast-weight memory. Building on this insight, we propose Select-TTT, a model-agnostic, query-conditioned rule for In-Place TTT that scales each chunk's write strength by its relevance to the user query. This relevance signal requires no supervision and no new parameters: it is read directly from the model's frozen output head, as the probability mass that a chunk's hidden states assign to the query tokens. A temperature-scaled softmax turns these scores into learning-rate multipliers, leaving the chunk-wise, context-parallel computation of In-Place TTT intact. Select-TTT consistently improves long-context question answering on two benchmarks and two backbones. These findings highlight the value of query-conditioned fast weight writes for practical test-time training on long-context question answering, which is also potentially promising for long-horizon agent tasks involving multiple user queries over evolving contexts. △ Less

Afterwards, a dual-prototype classifier combines featur… ▽ More Heterophilous graphs are prevalent in real-world applications, where connected nodes often exhibit dissimilar attributes or belong to different classes, posing significant challenges to conventional graph learning methods based on homophily. This limitation becomes particularly pronounced in generalized category discovery (GCD), which requires models to recognize known classes and discover novel ones using labels from known classes only. However, existing graph GCD methods typically rely on aforementioned homophily-based inductive biases, which are ill-suited to heterophilous graphs. Meanwhile, although current heterophilous graph learning methods exploit diverse neighborhood signals, their selection and fusion are often guided by supervision across all classes, including novel classes whose labels are unavailable in GCD. These limitations raise a key question: how can heterogeneous neighborhood information be effectively leveraged for category discovery without any novel-class supervision? To answer this question, in this study we propose HeteGCD, a new statistics-aware dual-prototype learning perspective for generalized category discovery on heterophilous graphs. The key idea is to examine both statistics of local attribute differences and neighborhood class distribution to extract discriminative information in feature and probability spaces simultaneously. Specifically, a gated dual-stream encoder integrates both node attributes and local attribute-difference statistics to learn informative node representations. Afterwards, a dual-prototype classifier combines feature-based similarity with distribution-based consistency to support category discovery. Experiments on five heterophilous graphs show that HeteGCD achieves competitive performance against 24 baseline combinations. △ Less

The model separates computation by modality: the target-agnostic audio encoder processes only the mixture, while the target-specific visual cues of the selected face condition only the decoder. For evaluation, we measure how quickly output quality recovers after a switch. On the standard LRS3-2mix test set, Tap2Listen improves SI-SNRi from 8.85 to 9.68 dB at a comparable parameter count. After a switch to a known face, the model produces full-quality output from the first frame, whereas the baseline needs about 1 second to recover. Finally, we show that the proposed streaming AV-TSE model runs in real time on a single CPU core. △ Less

It runs 1.3-7.9 times faster end-to-end than the most accurate competitor on each benchmark and scales to the largest benchmark graph. On graphs mixing two tasks, its node-level variant achieves the best AP among CGL methods. The design targets homophilic graphs with community structure. △ Less

    cs.LG

    The Batch Size Can Be One: Test-Time Adaptation from Classifier Geometry

    Authors: Jaeho Hwang, Yujin Oh, Juhyeon Shin, Juhye Lee, Sungroh Yoon, Uiwon Hwang

    Abstract: Test-time adaptation updates a deployed model on an unlabeled test stream. In practice this… ▽ More Test-time adaptation updates a deployed model on an unlabeled test stream. In practice this stream often arrives one sample at a time, with the classes in no particular order. A single sample then provides limited information for estimating distribution-level statistics, and selecting samples by confidence can admit predictions that are wrong yet confident. A corruption, however, shifts the features while leaving the decision regions of the frozen classifier unchanged. We therefore propose Origin-recentered Test-Time Adaptation (OriTTA), which constructs fixed geometric targets from the source model and adapts selected features toward them. Seen from the origin of the feature space, the classes lean toward a common direction. We choose a new origin to bring class scores closer together while keeping it near the mean feature of one synthetic prototype per class. From this origin, prototype directions spread more evenly, and each class obtains an axis and an ideal point before any test data arrives. During adaptation, OriTTA aligns selected features with their predicted class axis and penalizes excessive radius, keeps one input per predicted class in memory to limit feature changes for classes absent from the stream, and resets the model when re-classifying this memory reveals collapse. Without tuning for each setting, OriTTA achieves the highest mean accuracy at batch size 1 on CIFAR-10-C, CIFAR-100-C and ImageNet-C in all sixteen combinations of four backbones, two protocols and two stream orders. On ImageNet-C fog, recent methods collapse in up to two thirds of one hundred runs, while OriTTA never does. △ Less

    AOS-Bench: Can LLM Agents Manage Dynamic Workloads?

    Authors: Erle Zhu, Genrui Zhang, Keyang Hu, Mingyu Su, Hongning Wang

    Abstract: Coding agents are moving from handling one request at a time to serving as persistent assistants to which users hand a stream of work: a build, a data-processing job and a bug fix may all be pending on the same machine at once. Such an agent must decide when to take on new tasks, how to delegate and overlap them, and how to stay within the machine's CPU… ▽ More Coding agents are moving from handling one request at a time to serving as persistent assistants to which users hand a stream of work: a build, a data-processing job and a bug fix may all be pending on the same machine at once. Such an agent must decide when to take on new tasks, how to delegate and overlap them, and how to stay within the machine's CPU and memory. Existing benchmarks cannot measure this, because they evaluate each task in isolation. We introduce AOS-Bench, which evaluates an agent system as a workload manager: verified tasks arrive over time and must be delivered under shared CPU and memory limits, and the same tasks run one at a time serve as a per-system reference. Across five model--harness pairs, isolated task success does not carry over evenly. On heavy workloads every system loses 13 to 23 points. On medium workloads, systems span 11 points in isolated success but 26 points in stream success, and two systems 0.6 points apart in isolation deliver 54\% and 68\% of the same tasks. Execution-policy interventions trace much of this gap to task intake: serial dispatch leaves 73\% of heavy-workload tasks unclaimed. Yet more elaborate management does not necessarily help. A fixed concurrency of two comes within four points of the best agent-managed policy on both medium and heavy workloads, and under every policy median CPU use stays below 30\% of the provisioned cores while tasks wait. These results identify workload management as a distinct capability for agents, and AOS-Bench as a way to measure it. △ Less

    Recall or Relearn? A Mixture of In-Context Experts for Recurring Concept Drift

    Authors: Zhangkai Wu, Xu Zhang, Zhihong Cui, Yiqi Wang, Yi Zhang

    Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) are being applied to Web data streams such as network intrusion detection, where they adapt by updating the labelled rows of their context without retraining. When a past concept returns after leaving the context, a TFM faces a recall-or-relearn decision between restoring stored rows of the concept and relearning it from new… ▽ More Tabular foundation models (TFMs) are being applied to Web data streams such as network intrusion detection, where they adapt by updating the labelled rows of their context without retraining. When a past concept returns after leaving the context, a TFM faces a recall-or-relearn decision between restoring stored rows of the concept and relearning it from new labels. We propose MICE (Mixture of In-Context Experts), a training-free learner that cuts the stream into segments at detected changes. It merges a closed segment into a stored context when the TFM's estimated additional error on the segment is within a tolerance, under a capacity limit on stored contexts. A fast level weights the stored contexts and first-in-first-out (FIFO) windows by the log-loss of their predictions on the last labelled batch. We show that, with pure stored contexts and a margin condition, this fast level gains over relearning, in expected accuracy, the normalised positive area between the stored context's accuracy and the TFM's learning curve, up to a first-batch term. A slow level mixes the fast level's prediction with that of the FIFO window of the last labelled rows and bounds the discounted half-Brier regret of MICE against this window at every time. Learning curves measured beforehand predict the ordering of the observed recall gains. MICE outperforms reset, which empties the context at detected drifts, on grid streams with random boundary phases. Merging keeps fewer experts than pools that store every segment, and with fixed segmentation a time-matched snapshot pool matches MICE on the shifted grid. On real streams, MICE has the highest mean accuracy of all policies we compare. △ Less

Prior to… ▽ More We prove the first unconditional lower bound tradeoff between the number of passes and the approximation factor of maximum matchings in the semi-streaming model. Specifically, we show that any algorithm that outputs an $(1-\varepsilon)$-approximation to maximum matching requires $Ω(\log(1/\varepsilon))$ passes for constant $\varepsilon \in (0,1)$. Prior to our result, such tradeoffs were proven only conditioned on a certain combinatorial assumption about the density of Ruzsa-Szemerédi graphs. The state-of-the-art unconditional lower bound rules out any approximation factor better than $(8/9)$ in two passes but leaves open the possibility of a $(1-\varepsilon)$-approximation for any $\varepsilon > 0$ even in three passes. We provide two different proofs of our result: the first proof uses the recent hierarchical-embedding framework of Assadi, Konrad, Naidu, and Sundaresan (STOC 2024) along with combinatorial constructions using Ruzsa-Szemerédi graphs. The second extends the two-level Ruzsa-Szemerédi hierarchy of Konrad and Naidu (SODA 2024) beyond two levels and proves the corresponding communication lower bound directly by induction, rather than through a reduction to Index. △ Less

    ADTGuard: Adaptive Depth-Time Safeguard for Streaming Inference in Large Language Models

    Authors: Chuming Wei, Churui Yang, Zihan Zhang, Ruien Li, Wusheng Zhang, Yan Zhang, Guangwen Yang

    Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) can generate fluent responses to diverse user requests, yet streaming may expose users to harmful content before generation is complete. Existing guardrails face two limitations in this setting. First, post-hoc moderation at response or chunk boundaries delays intervention, risking harmful exposure when content is released before… ▽ More Large Language Models (LLMs) can generate fluent responses to diverse user requests, yet streaming may expose users to harmful content before generation is complete. Existing guardrails face two limitations in this setting. First, post-hoc moderation at response or chunk boundaries delays intervention, risking harmful exposure when content is released before verification. Second, even with token-level detection, coarse response-level supervision does not specify when harmful content begins, while fixed-layer readouts may miss relevant safety signals at other depths. To address these limitations, we introduce ADTGuard, which checks each token before release, progressively refines token-level training targets, and adapts layer selection to the current prefix. ADTGuard models risk across depth and time through Depth-Time Safety Dynamics (DTSD), which integrates safety evidence across layers at each token and uses it to guide risk accumulation over successive tokens. To adapt the choice of observation layers to the current prefix, we propose the Causal Depth Router (CDR). With only response-level labels available, we further develop Risk-Onset Target Refinement (RTR) to progressively construct token-level training targets for both components. Through extensive experiments across multiple safety benchmarks, we demonstrate that ADTGuard consistently achieves strong streaming detection performance, matching or surpassing state-of-the-art safeguards while requiring only 16M trainable parameters and maintaining competitive per-token latency. △ Less

    CASEL: Consequence-Aware Selective Execution for Reliable Long-Horizon Tool-Using Agents

    Authors: Yanyan Zhang, Fei Shen, Ting Yan, Hao Zhang

    Abstract: Tool-using language agents increasingly modify external systems, yet most runtime safeguards allocate scrutiny by generic uncertainty scores or coarse mutation heuristics. We introduce CASEL (Consequence-Aware Selective Execution Layer), a model-agnostic runtime allocation policy that combines calibrated failure probability, state-dependent consequence, reversibility, ambiguity, and evidence fresh… ▽ More Tool-using language agents increasingly modify external systems, yet most runtime safeguards allocate scrutiny by generic uncertainty scores or coarse mutation heuristics. We formalize direct execution as selective prediction and state and test the validity conditions of a finite-sample threshold and a trajectory risk ledger. In a controlled simulation over 20 seeds with 120,000 test actions each, in which every method has the same intervention menu and the same compute cost, CASEL reduces harmful-side-effect trajectories from 14.75 percent for an uncertainty-ranked gate to 7.02 percent and mean normalized harm from 0.184 to 0.123. A gate ranked by the product of failure probability and consequence reaches similar harm (5.82 percent, 0.120) but sends 45 percent more actions to human approval, and removing reversibility-aware routing raises the rate to 15.53 percent. A trajectory-level risk budget, in contrast, increases harm at equal cost (11.82 percent). Intervention effectiveness is assumed, not measured: the results rank allocation rules under the simulator's assumptions and do not establish deployment effectiveness. △ Less

    Authors: Aditi Sengupta, Sanjeev Kumar, Puneet Kumar

Vorticity-based proper or… ▽ More The flow past a rotating cylinder undergoes substantial changes in its wake dynamics and aerodynamic response with increasing rotation rate. This study investigates these changes in two-dimensional compressible flow at \(Re_\infty=3800\) and \(M_\infty=0.1\), with the rotation rate systematically varied over \(0.1\leqΩ\leq10\) using 130 high-fidelity simulations. At low rotation rates, conventional periodic vortex shedding is progressively suppressed with increasing rotation. A narrow transition regime around \(Ω=2.2-2.8\) is identified, where sustained oscillations evolve into intermittent fluctuations before reaching a nearly quiescent state. At higher rotation rates, unsteadiness re-emerges with distinctly intermittent and multiscale characteristics, demonstrating that this instability is fundamentally different from conventional Kármán shedding. The surface pressure and streamwise skin-friction distributions reveal substantial redistribution of aerodynamic loading with rotation, characterized by increasingly intense suction and significant modification of the near-wall shear field. The maximum lift increases systematically with rotation, reflecting the strengthening Magnus-Robins effect, whereas drag exhibits a non-monotonic response with enhanced variability at high rotation rates. Despite the low free-stream Mach number, rotational acceleration produces local Mach numbers approaching 0.5, indicating appreciable compressibility effects in the near-cylinder region. Vorticity-based proper orthogonal decomposition further reveals a transition from wake-dominated coherent structures at low rotation to strongly wrapped, rotation-dominated structures at high rotation. These results establish a direct connection between rotational forcing, surface loading, compressibility, and the suppression and subsequent re-emergence of flow unsteadiness. △ Less

However, effective program search requires physical understanding: agents must understand how objects can be manipulated, how scene geometry constrains actions, and why interactions succeed or fail. Existing methods rely mainly on execution feedback for program refinement, often leaving these physical questions unresolved. We introduce RoboAuto, a framework for robot program search that explicitly organizes physical understanding at the object, scene, and interaction levels through three components. First, a hierarchical knowledge base combines executable skills with object-centric knowledge of affordances, spatial relations, and action effects, learned from embodied data and online task solving. Second, scene grounding connects this knowledge to the current environment by identifying relevant objects and resolving action-critical quantities. Third, multi-stage planning with self-designed experiments checks whether each stage produces its expected physical effects and actively tests competing hypotheses to resolve physical uncertainties. RoboAuto achieves superior performance across five benchmark families, attaining an 89.1% success rate on short-horizon LIBERO-PRO tasks and more than twice the mean success rate of ASPIRE on LIBERO-PRO Long and LIBERO-Plus. It also outperforms ASPIRE and $π_{0.5}$ on three challenging long-horizon real-robot tasks requiring precise manipulation. We further show that the knowledge base transfers across simulators and embodiments, and that program search performance scales with its size. Project page: https://roboauto-ai.github.io/ △ Less

Sequence reconstruction combines noisy copies of each strand before downstream decoding. Conventional offline methods typically reconstruct completed read clusters, separating reconstruction from ongoing read collection. Streaming reconstruction offers a way to overlap evidence processing with sequencing and reduce the computation remaining before downstream decoding. A smaller public benchmark was completed in a fraction of a second. Parallel ACOR achieved speedups of tens to over a thousand-fold relative to the tested serial baseline configurations. Sparse storage reduced high-context memory requirements, while parallel execution accelerated reconstruction computation. ACOR is designed for streaming readout pipelines that process assigned reads while sequencing continues. △ Less

    HIDRA: Hierarchical Dual-Routing Attention for Replay-Free Lifelong Imitation Learning

    Authors: Fanqi Yu, Matteo Tiezzi, Cigdem Beyan, Tommaso Apicella, Vittorio Murino

    Abstract: Robotic agents operating in real-world environments must adapt continuously from a stream of multimodal demonstrations, often under constraints that preclude storing or revisiting past data. In this replay-free, single-pass lifelong imitation learning setting, sequential updates can progressively distort the representation space, making expert-based approaches particularly sensitive to routing interference, causing expert selection to degrade over time. We propose HIDRA, a structured routing framework that mitigates this issue through a hierarchical dual-attention mechanism, which decouples instruction-level expert retrieval from context-dependent refinement. To further stabilize routing under representation drift, HIDRA introduces a key-level geometric regularization that enforces both alignment within tasks and separation across tasks. The resulting approach enables reliable expert selection while supporting an expandable set of experts for continual adaptation. We evaluate HIDRA on several LIBERO benchmark suites, including more challenging variants with heterogeneous task sequences and paraphrased language instructions. Our approach consistently outperforms both replay-free and replay-based baselines, improving AUC and forward transfer while maintaining low forgetting, with stronger gains under high interference and language variation. The code is available at: https://github.com/yfqi/HIDRA. △ Less

Such worlds must track object states, apply interaction rules, and render the resulting consequences as users act. Yet existing end-to-end video world models typically learn interaction dynamics within the generator, making it difficult to specify object-level rules and reason about their consequences. In this work, we introduce BoxWorld, which separates interaction reasoning from generative rendering. Given user-specified rules, a coding agent creates a Box World Engine that determines interaction outcomes and exposes the resulting object states through a lightweight interface of layout boxes, persistent object attributes, and dynamic status variables, leaving appearance and physical effects to generative rendering. To this end, we introduce a three-stage training strategy that learns object grounding from open-domain videos and state transitions from gameplay videos, both annotated automatically, and finally performs rollout distillation on self-generated histories. We further propose PairedStateBench, a paired benchmark for fine-grained interaction control. BoxWorld substantially outperforms end-to-end world models and remains competitive across multiple public benchmarks. Project page: https://boxworld-web.github.io. △ Less

    Authors: Andrew Sheinberg, Samyak Gupta, Kai Li

    Abstract: …study reveals three key findings: (1) the GPU cluster exhibits significant underutilization, with over 70% of jobs using less than 20% of GPU memory and 50% averaging below 5% Streaming Multiprocessor (SM) utilization; (2) 80% of jobs have a drastic mismatch between their requested and actual durations, significantly limiting the scheduler's effectivenes… ▽ More GPU clusters have become essential computing resources for AI and scientific research in academia and industry. However, their high cost and growing demand often result in long queueing delays. While previous monitoring efforts have operated at second- or minute-level frequencies, they primarily informed job-level scheduling for clusters and lacked the detail needed to optimize GPU-level utilization. This paper presents an in-depth study of a university research GPU cluster, analyzing resource usage at 100 ms intervals. We have developed a lightweight monitoring tool that leverages Nvidia's DCGM library to capture fine-grained "compute utilization" metrics within GPUs. Deployed on a cluster with 89 nodes and 319 GPUs, our tool collected data over several months. This paper focuses on a 10-day period with 7,399 jobs submitted by 150 users from 12 departments. Our study reveals three key findings: (1) the GPU cluster exhibits significant underutilization, with over 70% of jobs using less than 20% of GPU memory and 50% averaging below 5% Streaming Multiprocessor (SM) utilization; (2) 80% of jobs have a drastic mismatch between their requested and actual durations, significantly limiting the scheduler's effectiveness; and (3) the multi-GPU jobs we examined show highly periodic communication patterns. These findings suggest there is plenty of room in the middle: substantial utilization gains are available through better job scheduling and GPU multitasking, independent of hardware or application improvements. Our monitoring solution is available as open-source software at https://github.com/als244/gpu_server_monitoring. △ Less

However, typically, the geometry is recovered by associating 2D pixels with 3D points, so a modular SLAM system absorbs tracking error into the map, leading to cascading degradation. Streaming spatial geometry replaces the per- frame cross-dimensional correspondence with a chunk-level metric prior that supervises tracking and mapping from one source. A ray-conditioned kernel extends each primitive with a viewing-direction subspace that senses the monocular camera viewpoints. The joint optimization over spatial geometry and poses ensures a globally consistent map and trajectory outcome. Experiments on the KITTI and Waymo Open datasets show that our SG^2SLAM surpasses the per-frame association baselines in the monocular outdoor navigation task. Code will be made publicly available upon acceptance. △ Less

    count-catcher: Lean Theorems from Model Counting Certificates

    Authors: Johannes K. Fichte, Stefan Szeider

    Abstract: …count-catcher checks in Lean that the formula implies the circuit (forward check) and the circuit implies the formula (reverse check). For the forward check, lrat-catcher streams a refutation through the LRAT checker of Lean's standard library. The reverse check and the validity check of the circuit are computations proved correct in Lean. Lean runs all… ▽ More We present count-catcher, a tool that turns a CPOG certificate of a count by the exact model counter d4 into a Lean theorem that a CNF formula defined in Lean has exactly N models over V variables. With an encoding lemma, this theorem becomes a theorem about the number of combinatorial objects. A CPOG certificate consists of a Boolean circuit equivalent to the formula and a proof of equivalence. count-catcher checks in Lean that the formula implies the circuit (forward check) and the circuit implies the formula (reverse check). For the forward check, lrat-catcher streams a refutation through the LRAT checker of Lean's standard library. The reverse check and the validity check of the circuit are computations proved correct in Lean. Lean runs all three checks as compiled code, so the theorem depends on Lean's kernel, compiler and runtime, as does CPOG's verified checker. We combine the theorem with results from Mathlib, for instance with Burnside's lemma to count graphs up to isomorphism. We prove the number of Latin squares of order 8 as a weighted sum of hundreds of certified counts from about eight terabytes of certificates. We turn about half of the 2022 Model Counting Competition formulas into theorems, with the three checks taking about a quarter of the time of CPOG's verified checker. We found that CPOG's unverified checker reported wrong counts, and removed its verified checker's dependence on two unproved lemmas. End to end, a theorem costs about the same as a checked count. △ Less

    VISTA: Differentiable Viewpoint Planning for Long-Horizon Task Visualization

    Authors: Donggang Jia, Da Li, Zainab Alsuwaykit, Sebastian Hartwig, Dominik Engel, Timo Ropinski, Ivan Viola

    Abstract: Long-… ▽ More Long-horizon object-centered tasks in interactive 3D environments involve heterogeneous events that unfold across time and space, including fine-grained manipulations, larger structural motions, and subtle state transitions. Effectively visualizing such processes is essential for helping users understand how actions lead to outcomes over time. However, existing camera control methods in 3D systems are commonly designed for static objects, and often fail to maintain the legibility of task-relevant components and their evolving relationships throughout long-horizon executions. We present VISTA, a differentiable viewpoint planning framework for long-horizon task visualization. We demonstrate that the same planner supports two complementary use cases: presentation-driven narrative visualization for communicating how long-horizon tasks unfold to human viewers, and agent-driven active observation within an observe-act loop.     Authors: Aritra Kundu, Robyn Sanderson, Nora Shipp, Adrien Thob, Adam Lidz, Nondh Panithanpaisal

    Abstract: The NSF-DOE Vera C. Rubin Observatory's Legacy Survey of Space and Time (LSST) will detect resolved stellar populations of intact dwarf galaxies and disrupted stellar streams in the Milky Way (MW) halo and beyond, opening a new avenue for near-field cosmology, particularly for constraining the faint galaxies during reionization. To interpret these data,… ▽ More The NSF-DOE Vera C. Rubin Observatory's Legacy Survey of Space and Time (LSST) will detect resolved stellar populations of intact dwarf galaxies and disrupted stellar streams in the Milky Way (MW) halo and beyond, opening a new avenue for near-field cosmology, particularly for constraining the faint galaxies during reionization. To interpret these data, we present and publicly release mock LSST catalogs of satellites, stellar streams, phase-mixed galaxies, and field galaxies around 13 MW-mass hosts in the FIRE-2 zoom-in simulations, generated with \textsf{py-ananke}. Using a surface brightness limit of $μ_{g} \leq 34$ $\rm{mag}$ $\rm{arcsec^{-2}}$, we find that $\approx29\%$ of streams with $M_{\star} \gtrsim 5 \times 10^5$ $M_{\odot}$ are detectable with tidal tails, while another $\approx38\%$ are detectable only through their bound progenitors and would appear as satellites. We estimate that $\approx96\%$ of satellites and $\approx64\%$ of field galaxies (out to $\sim$3.5 Mpc) with $M_{\star} \gtrsim 10^{5}$ $M_{\odot}$ are detectable, decreasing to $\approx$59\% and $\approx$42\% for $M_{\star} \gtrsim 10^{4.5}$ $M_{\odot}$ owing to resolution limits. Including streams detectable as satellites reproduces the radial distribution of observed MW satellites, suggesting that some observed satellites may be bound remnants of disrupted systems. Our mock galaxies match the sizes of observed satellites with $M_{\rm{V}} \lesssim -6$. Star-formation histories computed from only the detectable stars broadly recover the simulated star-formation histories. As an application, we test the near-far technique for reconstructing the proto-MW ultraviolet luminosity function (UVLF) during reionization ($z \gtrsim 6$). Adding the detectable streams improves the recovered faint-end slope at $z = 7$ and reduces the halo-to-halo scatter by $\approx33\%$. △ Less

    FOCUS: Active Critic Learning for Long-Horizon Language Model Agents

    Authors: Zhengping Jiang, William Jurayj, Liaoyaqi Wang, Chunsheng Zuo, Anqi Liu, Benjamin Van Durme

    Abstract: Sparse outcome rewards make it difficult for long-horizon language-model agents to assign credit to earlier actions. Learned critics provide value estimates for intermediate states, but these estimates can be inaccurate, while estimating intermediate values with repetitive continuation samples from points within a traj… ▽ More Sparse outcome rewards make it difficult for long-horizon language-model agents to assign credit to earlier actions. Learned critics provide value estimates for intermediate states, but these estimates can be inaccurate, while estimating intermediate values with repetitive continuation samples from points within a trajectory incurs substantial costs. To mitigate these issues, we introduce FOCUS, an RL method that uses selective branching to help the critic network learn its value estimator. The rollout controller facilitates active learning for the critic by sampling continuations from states that are expected to most reduce the critic's estimation error. These refined value estimates then score the segment between consecutive states with a TD($λ$) advantage, while the sampled evidence supplies training targets for the critic. FOCUS supports long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning through a modular context-management interface that decouples context management from rollout allocation. Our experiments show that using compaction-induced segment boundaries for value estimation and credit assignment, FOCUS outperforms GRPO and VinePPO with matched per-step generation budgets on legal reasoning, deep search, and code localization benchmarks, improving held-out accuracy at Qwen3.5-9B scale by 7.2 points over the strongest baseline on BrowseComp-Plus (55.4 vs. 48.2) and by 4.1 points on DeonticBench (56.5 vs. 52.4). △ Less

OmniBrain-0 combines strong cross-domain task performance with substantially lower inference and execution overhead than its closed-source teacher, demonstrating efficient transfer and consolidation of embodied capabilities into a compact unified agent. △ Less

    CLOSURE: Cross-Channel Closure for Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection

    Authors: Fan Xu, Hao Wu, Zhi Song, Hanbo Huang, Wenzhuo Ma, Nan Wang, Wei Gong, Xibin Zhao

    Abstract: …on normal data. Extensive experiments show that CLOSURE consistently outperforms recent reconstruction-, frequency-, and topology-based detectors on public benchmark streams, confirming the value of examining consistency alongside recoverability. ▽ More Multivariate time series produced by industrial installations and server clusters are under continuous monitoring, and anomaly detection is the first line of defense before a local fault escalates into a system-wide failure. In the absence of labels, the dominant paradigm trains a reconstruction model on normal data and scores the residual to reach a verdict. Recent work has steadily enriched the expressiveness of the recovery map through Transformers, diffusion models, and cross-scale associations. A residual, however, only certifies that an observation can be recovered; it does not certify that the variables still explain one another as they do under normal operation. A coordinated yet unfamiliar operating transition inflates the residual without breaking any relationship, whereas a sensor drifting away from its peers may leave the residual silent. Methods that fold inter-variable dependencies into the reconstruction objective inherit this ambiguity, because their relational evidence is ultimately supervised by the same recovery loss. We therefore propose CLOSURE, which evaluates recoverability and cross-channel consistency as two normal-data prediction tasks learned in separation. The recoverability task is solved by a diffusion reconstructor conditioned on a discrete memory of normal temporal bases. The consistency task is solved by a relation-closure operator that predicts every channel exclusively from the others, where the target's own trajectory is withheld and sparse signed gates decide which peers are actually used. The two scores are reconciled only through calibration on normal data. Extensive experiments show that CLOSURE consistently outperforms recent reconstruction-, frequency-, and topology-based detectors on public benchmark streams, confirming the value of examining consistency alongside recoverability. △ Less

This paper proposes a category-selective encryption framework for privacy-preserving UAV surveillance. The core advance over prior class-agnostic approaches is semantic-level selectivity: the system first recognizes what is present in the scene via an 80-class detector, then encrypts only user-specified sensitive categories while leaving non-sensitive objects and background intact for downstream analytics.     MiRL: Error-Targeted Reinforcement Learning for Correcting Terminal Agent Misbehaviors

    Authors: Runchu Tian, Zaid Khan, Zun Wang, Justin Chih-Yao Chen, Mohit Bansal

    Abstract: …of the model, train the model to fix these weaknesses, and check whether the weaknesses are fixed. We study how to apply error-targeted learning to terminal agents, which solve long-… ▽ More Error-targeted learning gives a legible way to improve a model: identify specific weaknesses of the model, train the model to fix these weaknesses, and check whether the weaknesses are fixed. We study how to apply error-targeted learning to terminal agents, which solve long-horizon tasks specified in natural language by interacting with a command shell. Existing error-targeted methods are designed for single-turn tasks, and existing analyses of agent failures are used to evaluate and debug agents rather than to train them. We propose Misbehavior-driven RL (MiRL), which first profiles the errors of a policy to find generalizable misbehaviors: kinds of wrong steps that recur across many environments. MiRL then trains the policy with RL, starting rollouts at the steps where these misbehaviors occur and rewarding a rollout only if it passes the tests and avoids the misbehavior. On Qwen3.5-4B, eight misbehaviors account for 52.1% of wrong steps. After training with MiRL, the policy repeats the measured misbehaviors 43% to 57% less often on average on unseen prefixes. MiRL outperforms TMax-4B on Terminal-Bench Lite (51.8% vs. 47.4%) and matches it on Terminal-Bench 2.1 (21.3% vs. 20.6%), while using 35% of its rollouts. These results show that error-targeted learning can be applied to realistic, long-horizon terminal tasks. △ Less

    Explainable Prediction of High-Speed Solar Wind Streams driven by Coronal Holes via Topological Data Analysis

    Authors: Francesco Marchetti, Sabrina Guastavino, Daniel Collin

    Abstract: High-speed solar wind streams (HSSs) originating from coronal holes (CHs) are an important driver of recurrent geomagnetic activity. Since HSSs are associated to coronal structures visible in extreme-ultraviolet observations, this provides a natural basis for forecasting several days in advance. In this work, we investigate whether the topology of coronal structures observed in extreme ultraviolet (EUV) images contains predictive information on the subsequent occurrence of CH-driven HSSs at Earth. To this end, we consider Topological Data Analysis (TDA), and in particular persistent homology, to classify EUV solar images according to the occurrence of HSSs at Earth four days later. The target is a binary classification of HSS occurrence at a four-day lead time, defined using an HSS interval catalog and solar wind speed thresholds. Several combinations of topological representations and classifiers are evaluated under a common cross-validation framework. The results indicate that the topology of EUV coronal structures alone contains predictive information on subsequent CH-driven HSS occurrence, despite the absence of explicit CH geometric descriptors in the model input. To investigate the origin of this predictive information, we develop a model-level explainability framework based on cross-validation-aggregated feature weights and spatial evidence maps. A fully traced example connects persistence diagrams and persistence-image features to the classifier output and to their spatial localization in the original EUV observation. The proposed framework provides a transparent and computationally lightweight approach for investigating the relationship between coronal topology and subsequent high-speed solar wind streams. △ Less

    WorldPager: Zero-Stall Tiered Memory for Long-Horizon World Models on a Single Consumer GPU

    Authors: Bingnan Liu, Ruini Xue

    Abstract: Interactive world models keep the KV of past chunks as retrieval memory so that revisited places stay consistent. On a single consumer machine (one RTX 4090, 32 GiB of host memory, one NVMe SSD), long sessions with such memory hit two walls. On a single consumer machine (one RTX 4090, 32 GiB of host memory, one NVMe SSD), long sessions with such memory hit two walls. The first is capacity: the KV of Matrix-Game 2.0 exhausts host memory after 45 seconds of video in bf16 and after 2.8 minutes in int4. The second is the model's positional horizon of 4.3 minutes. We show that this memory is capacity-bound rather than bandwidth-bound and that its access set is known before compute, because the camera pose is an input. An analytical model built on these observations predicts spill points to within one chunk and classifies zero versus non-zero stalls correctly on held-out configurations. Guided by it, WorldPager stores int4 KV in a GPU/DRAM/NVMe hierarchy instead of recomputing it, prefetches each chunk's memory from the extrapolated pose, never waits on a miss, and writes back in the background; to our knowledge, it is the first to place the KV memory of an interactive world model on an NVMe tier with zero-stall prefetching. With training-free position handling, it generates 37.5 minutes of video continuously with zero stalls and constant host memory, at about 9.5 frames per second. On a sealed test set, int4 storage shows no measurable quality loss; on long-gap revisits it lowers image quality by about one MUSIQ point. △ Less

    Governing Enterprise AI Traffic at Scale: Design and Operation of a Policy-Driven, Multi-Vendor LLM Gateway on Envoy

    Authors: Mithun Sasidharan, Srinivasan Manoharan, Rupesh Thakur, Abhinav TK, Ashwin Hegde, Anand Gautam

    Abstract: Large organizations consume large language models (LLMs) from several vendors at once, each with its own API dialect, credential model, streaming protocol and pricing. Integrated directly, this heterogeneity yields credential sprawl, format lock-in, unbounded spend and inconsistent governance. We present the design and production operation of the Cosmos LLM Gateway, the governed entry point for enterprise LLM traffic at PayPal. Built on Envoy, it splits work by latency profile: I/O-bound control decisions (authentication, credential brokering, policy and budget evaluation) run in an out-of-process external-processing service, while wire-format translation, token metering and prompt-cache shaping run in-proxy as a Rust WebAssembly filter, so that, by default, streamed response body chunks never wait on an out-of-process call. Models, providers and prices are largely validated data rather than code. Governance is declarative policy evaluated by Open Policy Agent and managed through a control plane with role-scoped authoring, approvals, pilots, shadow-mode rollout, usage notifications and versioned policy bundles. For use cases that require it, a data-loss-prevention layer can block, redact or pseudonymize detected sensitive content before it reaches a provider. Spend scopes are views over per-model usage counters, so a new budget applies to spend already recorded in its window without migration. As of September 2026, the gateway serves more than 12,000 developers and hundreds of business use cases, handling roughly 200 billion tokens per day across more than 100 models at 99.999% availability to business. In a benchmark of the core request path against a synthetic upstream, it adds a median of 4 ms and a p99 of 11 ms of overhead at more than 2,200 requests per second on two 4-vCPU instances. We report the architecture, policy model, evaluation and lessons learned. △ Less

    Authors: Lei Zhao, Zongheng Cao, Xinyu Hu

    Abstract: …credit assignment improves learning and when it fails to improve task performance. We construct four movie-trailer editing task types---Content Selection, Narrat… ▽ More Reinforcement learning (RL) has shown promise for training multimodal agents to reason and use tools. However, it remains unclear when fine-grained credit assignment improves learning and when it fails to improve task performance. Decaying process rewards provides the largest benefit on Fine Cut, with a 45.4% relative gain over outcome-only GRPO on tasks averaging more than 60 turns under outcome-only GRPO. On the three shorter-horizon task types averaging 30--40 turns under the same baseline, outcome-only GRPO remains competitive, with decaying process rewards yielding at most a 10.4% relative gain. Process rewards from an LLM judge initially improve tool use and encourage visual inspection. As training progresses, however, GRPO+PRM with a fixed process-reward weight increasingly exhibits excessive reasoning and redundant tool calls: process rewards continue to rise while task completion rates and outcome scores decline. Decaying the process-reward weight to zero mitigates this reward-hacking behavior while preserving the early gains. Despite dense token-level supervision, on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) yields little improvement or substantial degradation and is highly sensitive to the choice of privileged information. Our analysis reveals differences in teacher--student disagreement and gradient magnitudes, and suggests possible dilution of informative signals over long trajectories. △ Less

    Authors: Yinsong Yan, Ling Wang, Yuxuan Wang, Yifan Yang, Ruiyang Xu, Junming Lin, Qi Chen, Meng Gao, Minghao Han, Yunfei Chu, Qize Yang, Xize Cheng, Jin Xu, Jibin Wu

However, existing evaluations provide limited insight into how reliably these models connect stream evidence and user needs to timely, sustained interventions. We introduce \textbf{Omni-InteractScope}, a benchmark of 1,479 human-annotated, cross-reviewed interactions with 1,790 temporal targets across eight task groups. It links input modalities, event triggers, intervention types, and user needs, covering event-timed tool invocation and query-free assistance alongside notifications, warnings, state tracking, and continuous responses. Our experimental results on the proposed benchmark reveal three critical limitations of current Omni-LLMs: (1) \textbf{Timely tool invocation remains unreliable}: all 12 evaluated models produce an on-time first response for at most 40\% of tool-invocation targets, and joint timing-and-tool accuracy reaches at most 32.0\%. (2) \textbf{Query-free reminders remain poorly covered}: all evaluated models achieve lower timely reference-target recall on query-free reminders than on explicit questions, measured on their respective target sets. Reminder recall never exceeds 23.0\%. (3) \textbf{End-to-end guidance coverage remains limited}: nine models cover none of the 26 guidance sequences in full, and the remaining three cover only one to three sequences each. Targeted controls further show that tool-invocation scores alone do not establish event grounding and that reminder coverage depends on response history and frequency. These findings motivate developing and evaluating streaming assistants for timely action, initiative under general user needs, and coverage of entire response sequences. △ Less

    Toward Open ISA Supercomputing: A Comparative Study of RISC-V, Arm, and x86 Architectures on HPC Workloads

    Authors: Emanuele Venieri, Giacomo Madella, Gabriele Ceccolini, Simone Manoni, Federico Ficarelli, Filippo Barbari, Jenny Lynn Almerol, Elisabetta Boella, Nitin Shukla, Simone Bnà, Luca Benini, Andrea bartolini

    Abstract: …software stack and methodology. We characterize the Sophgo SG2044 and compare it with the NVIDIA Grace CPU Superchip and Intel Xeon Platinum 8480+ across four HPC benchmarks (STREAM, HPL, HPL-MxP, and HPCG) and four representative applications (iPIC3D, PLUTO, OpenFOAM, and llama$.$cpp). The version of HPL-MxP linked to OpenBLAS built and used for this work i… ▽ More RISC-V is emerging as a candidate architecture for high-performance computing (HPC), but its competitiveness against established x86 and Arm processors remains difficult to assess because existing studies use different platforms, workloads, and methodologies, and rarely consider energy efficiency. Our work provides an evaluation of contemporary HPC RISC-V, Arm, and x86 processors using a common software stack and methodology. We characterize the Sophgo SG2044 and compare it with the NVIDIA Grace CPU Superchip and Intel Xeon Platinum 8480+ across four HPC benchmarks (STREAM, HPL, HPL-MxP, and HPCG) and four representative applications (iPIC3D, PLUTO, OpenFOAM, and llama$.$cpp). The version of HPL-MxP linked to OpenBLAS built and used for this work is made available as open-source software. The SG2044 reaches 8.02 GFLOP/s of FP64 performance and 7.8 GB/s of memory bandwidth per core, increasing to 294 GFLOP/s and 107 GB/s at 64 cores. L2-cache-aware process placement enables near-linear scaling up to 16 cores, beyond which memory-bandwidth saturation limits further gains. Across generations of RISC-V hardware, in the context of the Monte Cimone (MC) project, the SG2044-based MCv3 doubles both single-core performance and peak memory bandwidth relative to MCv2, while improving memory bandwidth, node-level FP64 performance, and energy efficiency by approximately 100x, 158x, and 10x, over MCv1. Against the Arm and x86 systems, the SG2044 shows 8-15x less absolute performance, reflecting its narrower vector resources and less advanced technology node, but delivers competitive scaling and energy efficiency on several workloads. Its most notable result is LLM decode, where 64-core execution provides the highest relative scaling and energy efficiency among the evaluated platforms while exceeding Intel in throughput. Our results show that RISC-V processors are closing the gap with the competition. △ Less

    cs.CE cs.AI

    MarkovSim: an LLM Agent Harness with Bounded Context for Long-Horizon Computational Simulation

    Authors: Yeui-Lung Lei, Wen-Bo Chen, Pan Zhang

    Abstract: Recent studies have applied large language model (LLM) agent systems to computational simulation workflows. Such workflows are long-… ▽ More Recent studies have applied large language model (LLM) agent systems to computational simulation workflows. Such workflows are long-horizon, so the information generated can exceed what an LLM can reason over at once, its context window. Context management is therefore essential, yet existing systems have either neglected it or treated it as a supporting mechanism rather than a subject of study. This paper presents MarkovSim, an LLM agent harness for long-horizon computational simulation, whose core is a context management scheme built into the workflow. The workflow record is written to a memory system with a predefined schema, and at workflow-defined boundaries, namely the state transitions of a finite-state machine a compact representation of the solution progress is constructed from it as the agent's context. Instantiated on finite element analysis and evaluated on six benchmark problems, MarkovSim passed 16 of 18 runs and corrected its own faults in both the solver operations and the model physics. The engineer was consulted only where engineering judgement was needed. The context remained bounded throughout every run, regardless of its length. △ Less

    Comments: 26 pages,15 figures,not yet submitted to any journal

    MSC Class: 65-04 ACM Class: I.2.11; J.6

    One Megakernel is Not Enough: Predicting Where to Cut for Streaming Inference

    Authors: Aditya Chatterjee, Anand Joseph X M, Sanjay Singh Chauhan

    Abstract: …of multiple megakernels with no overlapping conventional graph component, or a fully conventional graph. We present a framework that selects megakernel regions in a streaming inference model from conventional-graph profiles and standalone kernel benchmarks, without running any candidate design end to end. It simulates each candidate's tile and event grap… ▽ More We address a fundamental question: How far should kernel fusion extend and which boundaries are worth preserving? Unlike the general convention of using one monolith megakernel at lower batch sizes or supporting only a selective model component as megakernel and rest of the model as conventional graph, we show that the best configuration is a composition of multiple megakernels with no overlapping conventional graph component, or a fully conventional graph. We present a framework that selects megakernel regions in a streaming inference model from conventional-graph profiles and standalone kernel benchmarks, without running any candidate design end to end. It simulates each candidate's tile and event graphs to predict time to first output and selects the measured best design in all 13 combinations of model and batch size we tested. Across NH-TTS, Qwen3-TTS and Llama-1B, the best composition ranges from two to four megakernels to fully conventional execution and none is a single megakernel. We introduce four model categories (megakernel-stable/ receding/ advancing/ averse) that capture how a model's best megakernel composition changes with batch size. Using NH-TTS model on H100 NVL GPU, we demonstrate that preserving three specialized megakernels can improve both latency and throughput despite introducing additional kernel boundaries. At batch size 32, this design delivers 21.4% lower mean batch-p95 time to first audio (TTFA) and 28% higher throughput than the evaluated monolith megakernel. Against conventional TensorRT graph execution, it reduces engine-level mean batch TTFA p95 by 4.05x and increases throughput by 19.7%. △ Less

    The Intermediate Silicon Tracker Detector for sPHENIX

    Authors: Y. Akiba, H. Aso, J. T. Bertaux, D. Cacace, R. G. Cecato, K. Y. Chen, S. Y. Chen, K. Y. Cheng, A. Enokizono, H. Enyo, K. Fujiki, Y. Fujino, M. Fujiiwara, T. Hachiya, T. Harada, S. Hasegawa, M. Hata, B. Hong, J. Hwang, T. Ichino, M. Ikemoto, D. Imagawa, H. Imai, Y. Ishigaki, M. Isshiki , et al. (51 additional authors not shown)

    Abstract: …mm. The INTT has been fully integrated into the sPHENIX data acquisition system, with detector signals processed by eight FELIX servers. Continuous data taking is enabled by a streaming readout mode, in which detector data are accumulated without hardware triggers, while a conventional trigger-based mode is also supported. The detector achieves stable operat… ▽ More A new barrel-type silicon detector has been constructed in the central rapidity region of the sPHENIX experiment as part of its charged-particle tracking system. The detector, referred to as the intermediate silicon tracker (INTT), provides single-event timing capability and contributes to charged-particle tracking in combination with the silicon-pixel vertex detector and the time-projection chamber. The INTT consists of two cylindrical barrels with full azimuthal coverage. The inner and outer barrels comprise 24 and 32 silicon strip ladders, respectively. Each ladder consists of four silicon strip sensors, covering the pseudorapidity range $|η| < 1.07$ for collision vertices within $\pm~10$ cm along the beam axis, at radial distances between 72 and 103 mm. The INTT has been fully integrated into the sPHENIX data acquisition system, with detector signals processed by eight FELIX servers. Continuous data taking is enabled by a streaming readout mode, in which detector data are accumulated without hardware triggers, while a conventional trigger-based mode is also supported. The detector achieves stable operation, small inactive area, and high efficiency, providing precise timing, tracking, and vertexing performance during the collection of beam-beam collision data. △ Less
